रा

T" Report has been prepared for submission to the Governor under
Article 151 of the Constitution.

Chapter 1 of this report indicates audited entity profile, authority for audit,
planning and conduct of audit, organisational structure of office of the
Principal Accountant General (Civil Audit) and response of the
Departments to the draft paragraphs. Significant audit observations
included in this report have also been brought out in this Chapter.

Chapter 2 deals with the findings of performance audit while Chapter 3
covers audit of transactions in various departments including autonomous
bodies. Chapter 4 includes a report on the CCO based audit of Horticulture
and Food Processing Department.

The cases mentioned in this Report are among those which came to notice in
the course of test audit of accounts during the year 2010-11 as well as those
which had come to notice in earlier years but could not be dealt with in
previous Reports; matters relating to the period subsequent to 2010-11 have
also been included, wherever necessary.



INTRODUCTION

This Report of the Comptroller and Auditor General of India (C&AG) relates
to matters arising from compliance audit of the transactions of the Civil and
Works departments of the Government of Uttar Pradesh, Centrally sponsored
and State plan schemes and audit of autonomous bodies of the State, including
performance audit of selected schemes and departments.

The primary purpose of the Report is to bring to the notice of the State
Legislature, important results of audit. Auditing standards require that the
materiality level of reporting should be commensurate with the nature, volume
and magnitude of transactions. The findings of audit are expected to enable the
Executive to take the corrective action as also to frame policies and directives
that lead to improved financial management of the organisations, thus
contributing to better governance.

Compliance audit refers to examination of the transactions relating to
expenditure, receipts, assets and liabilities of the audited entities to ascertain
whether the provision of the Constitution of India, applicable laws, rules and
regulations and various orders and instructions issued by the competent
authorities are being complied with.

Performance audit is an independent assessment or examination of the extent
to which an organisation, programme or scheme operates economically,
efficiently and effectively.

This Chapter provides the audited entity profile, the planning and extent of
audit. Chapter 2 of this Report deals with the findings of performance audit
and Chapter 3 deals with compliance audit in the various departments and
autonomous bodies. Chapter 4 contains results of CCO based audit of
Horticulture and Food Processing Department.

There are 65 departments at the Secretariat level, headed by Chief
Secretary/Principal Secretaries/Secretaries who are assisted by the Special
Secretaries, Deputy Secretaries and Directors and subordinates officers under
them and 27 autonomous bodies which are under audit jurisdiction of the
Principal Accountant General (Civil Audit), Uttar Pradesh, Allahabad.

The comparative position of expenditure of the Government during 2010-11
and in the preceding two years is given in Table 1.

Authority for audit by the C&AG is derived from the Articles 149 and 151 of
the Constitution of India and the Comptroller and Auditor General's (Duties,
Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. Principal Accountant General
(Civil Audit) conducted audit of expenditure of civil and works departments,
autonomous bodies under sections 13, 14, 15, 19 and 20 of the Comptroller &
Auditor General’s (Duties, Powers and Conditions of Service) Act, 1971. The
principles and methodology for compliance audit are prescribed in the
manuals issued by the Comptroller & Auditor General of India.

Under the directions of the C&AG, the Principal Accountant General (Civil
Audit), Uttar Pradesh, conducts audit of civil and works departments and
autonomous bodies through two groups each for inspection of the civil and
works departments, one group each for autonomous bodies and forest
department. During 2010-11, 71 audit parties conducted compliance audit of



Chapter I: Introduction
रा

the selected units under various civil and works departments of the State
Government, autonomous bodies, externally aided projects etc.

Audit process commences with the assessment of risk of various Government
departments/organisations/autonomous bodies and schemes/projects ete.,
based on expenditure, criticality/complexity of activities, level of delegated
financial powers, assessment of internal control and the concerns of
stakeholders. Previous audit findings are also considered in this exercise.

After completion of audit of each unit, Inspection Reports containing audit
findings are issued to the head of the unit/department. The units are requested
to furnish replies to the audit findings within one month of receipt of the
Inspection Report. Whenever replies are received, audit findings are either
settled or further action for compliance is advised. The important audit
observations arising out of these Inspection Reports are processed for
inclusion in the audit report.

During 2010-11, 16,612 party-days were used to carry out audit of 2,378 units
out of 6,940 units of various departments/organisations. The audit plan
covered those units/entities which were vulnerable to significant risk, as per
the assessment.

Performance audit is undertaken to ensure whether the Government
programmes have achieved the desired objectives at the minimum cost and
given the intended benefits.

This report contains results of Performance audits of Construction,
Modernisation and Maintenance of State Tubewells; Information System
Audit on 'Preparedness of Computer Based Management Information System
in Irrigation Department; and Long Paragraphs on Status of Infrastructure
Facilities and Teaching Aids in Government Primary and Upper Primary
Schools in Ghazipur and Gautam Buddha Nagar Districts and Status of
Medical Equipment and Apparatus in Ghazipur and Gautam Buddha Nagar
districts. The salient features of the performance audits are discussed below.

1.6.1 Performance Audit on “Construction, Modernisation and
Maintenance of State Tubewells”
Economy of Uttar Pradesh is dependent mainly on agriculture which provides
livelihood to 65 per cent of its population. Of total net irrigated area of 1.31
crore hectare in the State, tubewells contribute irrigation to only 0.04 crore
hectare (3 per cent) area. Performance audit on “Construction, Modernisation
and Maintencne of State Tubwells”, covering the period 2006-11, revealed
creation of liability of interest bearing loan. There were cases of procurement
of machines without requirement and without obtaining administrative and


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
—_ 34 94 आह 4991 3३3७3), ४३333.30 43.

financial sanction from the Government. Benefit Cost Ratio of the projects
was deliberately made economically viable. Huge liability was pending on
account of electricity charges. Despite investment of huge amount, the
Department could utilise only 27 per cent of the created irrigation potential.
The Government adopted fixed rate for electricity charges instead of opting
for metered billing which would have been beneficial for the Department.

1.6.2 Information System Audit on 'Preparedness of Computer Based

Management Information System in Irrigation Department’
Uttar Pradesh Irrigation Department (UPID) initiated a computer based
Management Information System (MIS) in April 2001, which was to be fully
implemented within 54 months. However, even after expiry of about 10 years,
the implementation of computer based Management Information System in the
Irrigation Department was far from completion. The objectives of effective
planning and analyses based on the factual data; increased monitoring;
enhanced transparency and communication, could not be achieved due to non
preparedness of the Department in making current data available to the
vendor, poor internet connectivity and inadequate training. Till date, not even
a single module, out of 26 modules, has been put to use. Even master data had
been fed partially and that too was unreliable. Department also failed to
develop a system to protect the data from any disaster.

1.6.3 Status of Infrastructure Facilities and Teaching Aids in Government
Primary and Upper Primary Schools in Ghazipur and Gautam
Buddha Nagar Districts

“UP Education for All Project Board” was constituted in May 1993 to manage

the World Bank assisted project and the powers to manage the primary and

upper primary schools were vested with the “Village Education Committees

(VEC).” Universalisation of elementary education was affected due to non-

availability of sufficient number of teachers; inadequate infrastructure

facilities like toilets, separate toilets for girls, safe and adequate drinking
water, mid-day-meal (MDM), sports grounds, computer facilities, seating
arrangements, free text books, uniforms, training of teachers, library facilities
and electrification in the Government Primary and Upper Primary Schools.

Despite availability of sufficient class rooms, improved Teacher Student

Ratio, MDM and other facilities, the Government was not able to enhance

enrolment, contain or restrict the dropout rate of children from the schools.

1.6.4 Status of medical equipment and apparatus in Ghazipur and Gautam
Buddha Nagar districts

The status of Medical equipment and apparatus in Ghazipur and Gautam
Buddha Nagar districts for 2006-11 was evaluated in audit. It revealed that a
large number of equipment was either not optimally utilised or was lying idle
mainly due to non-posting of medical staff. The bed occupancy was very low
and the patients were left to take recourse to private diagnostic centres. The


Chapter I: Introduction

ambulances were also not used for incoming and discharged patients. Huge
expenditure incurred on ventilators and ambulances was also proved to be
unproductive.

1.6.5 Chief Controlling Officer based audit of Horticulture and Food
Processing Department

The vision of the Horticulture and Food Processing Department is to increase
the productivity and quality of horticulture crops. CCO based audit of the
Department revealed that due to inadequate manpower and infrastructure and
non-utilisation of available funds, the objectives were largely unfulfilled.
Annual Action Plans were prepared without ascertaining the field level
requirements. Sincere efforts to establish nurseries for producing qualitative
planting materials and seeds were not made. Post Harvest Management,
essential for improving marketability of horticulture produce, was not given
due attention. The monitoring and evaluation system was inadequate, resulting
in poor execution of projects.

Audit observed significant deficiencies in critical areas, which impact the
effectiveness of the State Government. Some important findings of the
compliance audit (28 paragraphs) have also been reported. The major
observations relate to:

e Non-compliance with rules and regulations;

. Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate
justification;

न Failure of oversight/governance;

e Persistent and pervasive irregularities; and

e Fraud and detection of fraud.

1.7.1 Non-compliance with rules and regulations

For sound financial administration and control, it is essential that expenditure
conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders issued by the competent
authority. This helps in maintaining financial discipline and prevents
irregularities, misappropriation and frauds. This section of non-compliance
with rules and regulations brings out observations involving = 2765.23 crore.
Some of these are shown as under.

e Transfer of 501.922 acre land to the New Okhla Industrial Development
Authority without realising its full value led to a loss of €& 2,632.19
crore.

(Paragraph 3.1.1)

heritage building in Lucknow and in its place constructed a new one at
₹ 28.30 crore in disregard of the Ancient Monuments and Archeological
Sites and Remains Act, 1958.

(Paragraph 3.1.2)

e — Non-adherence to specifications resulted in not only sub-standard works
worth हैं 2.50 crore but also avoidable expenditure हैं 2.82 crore on
renewal works of village roads.

(Paragraph 3.1.4)

e Payment of secured advances, in violation of the contract conditions, led

₹ 2.23 crore as interest.

(Paragraph 3.1.6)

1.7.2 Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate
justification

Authorisation of expenditure from public funds has to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as
a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money.
Audit scrutiny revealed instances of impropriety and extra expenditure
amounting to = 121.33 crore. Some of the important audit findings are as
under.

e Laser Land Levelers, purchased at a cost of % 2.49 crore, without
assessing their feasibility, were lying idle, rendering the expenditure
incurred thereon unfruitful. Besides, the objective of ensuring
appropriate and minimum use of water in irrigating the agriculture
fields was also not achieved.

(Paragraph 3.2.1)

e = Appointment of lecturers of physical education and sports without
introducing the required curriculum resulted in wasteful expenditure of
% 6.54 crore defeating the objective of participation of youth of the State
in National and International sports events.

(Paragraph 3.2.4)

e —_Inviolation of the orders of the Supreme Court, Ghaziabad Development
Authority placed orders for supply of electrical items worth हैं 15.92
crore for development of site proposed for construction of houses
resulting in the items lying unused for about two years.

(Paragraph 3.2.5)

e Excess payment of हैं 3.48 crore was made to the contractor due to
preparation of estimate by allowing inadmissible Schedule of Rates.

(Paragraph 3.2.6)

the area of health, education, development and upgradation of infrastructure,
public services, etc. Audit noticed instances where the funds released by the
Government for creating public assets, remained unutilised/ blocked or proved
strative oversight

unfruitful/ unproductive due to indecisiveness, lack of 8017
and concerted action at various levels. Cases of failure of

. Cost of land allotted for housing schemes was not realised even after

1.7.4 Persistent and pervasive irregularity

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year after year. It is deemed
pervasive when prevalent in the entire system. Recurrence of irregularities,
despite being pointed out in earlier audits is indicative of slackness on the part


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
—_ 34 94 आह 4991 3३3७3), ४३333.30 43.

of the executive and lack of effective monitoring. This in turn encourages
willful deviations from observance of rules/regulations and results in
weakening of administrative structure. Audit observed that

न Execution of construction works of Rapti Link Channel (RLC) in
violation of the orders of the Government led to unauthorised and
unfruitful expenditure of हैं 2.21 crore.

(Paragraph 3.4.1)

1.7.5 Fraud and detection of fraud

Audit of financial transactions, test checked in Public Works Department and
their field functionaries revealed that

e Failure to verify the genuineness of the transfer order by the Executive
Engineer, Construction Division, Bridges and Roads, Gorakhpur, as
ordered by the Engineer-in-Chief, Public Works Department, not only
facilitated the joining of two fake employees but also resulted in
fraudulent drawl of salaries of हैं 3.73 lakh.

(Paragraph 3.5.1)

The draft performance reviews and audit paragraphs are forwarded to the
Principal Secretaries’ Secretaries of the concerned departments drawing their
attention to the audit findings and requesting them to send their responses
within six weeks. It is brought to their personal attention that in view of likely
inclusion of such paragraphs in the Audit Reports of the Comptroller and
Auditor General of India, which are placed before Uttar Pradesh Legislature, it
would be desirable to include their comments in the matter. They are also
advised to have meetings with the Principal Accountant General to discuss the
performance reviews /draft audit paragraphs, proposed for Audit Reports.

During May to December 2011, three draft performance reviews and 34 draft
paragraphs which included two long paragraphs were forwarded to the
concerned Principal Secretaries/Secretaries of the departments demi-officially.
The meetings for discussions were also arranged during October to December
2011.The responses in respect of all the draft performance reviews and
paragraphs were received and have suitably been incorporated in the Report.
However, Government/Departments did not furnish detail replies in respect of
Information System audit of Irrigation Department, CCO based audit of
Horticulture and Food Processing Department and both the long paragraphs.


Chapter II

PERFORMANCE AUDIT

This chapter contains results of performance audit of Construction,
Modernisation and Maintenance of State Tubewells: Information System
Audit on 'Preparedness of Computer Based Management Information System
in brigation Department’ and Long Paragraphs on Status of Infrastructure

districts.

[1001४ DEPARTMENT

2.1 Performance Audit on “Construction, Modernisation and
Maintenance of State Tubewells”

Executive Summary

Economy of Uttar Pradesh is dependent mainly on agriculture which provides
livelihood to 65 per cent of its population. Of total net irrigated area of 1.31
crore hectare in the State, tubewells contribute irrigation to only 0.04 crore
hectare (3 per cent) area. Performance audit of “Construction, Modernisation
and Maintenace of State Tubwells” disclosed the following:

« Failure of proper and required scrutiny of the project cost at E-in-C as
well as at the Government level, led to avoidable creation of liability of
interest bearing loan of हैं 1.60 crore.

© The Department purchased heavy machines costing € 3.06 crore without
obtaining administrative and financial sanction from the Government.

¢ Liability of € 177.06 crore was pending on account of electricity charges.

e The Department could utilise only 27 per cent of created irrigation
potential due to wrong assessment of failed STWs, improper selection of
sites for construction of new STWs, non-construction of field channels and
new pump sets were replaced where pump sets were functioning
effectively.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

2.1.1 Introduction

The Irrigation Department (ID) is responsible for investigation, planning,
designing, construction and maintenance of State Tubewell (STWs) Projects in
the State. ID started construction of STWs of capacity of 1.5 cusec, with a
command area of 100 hectares, in 1936. In 1999, operation of STWs with
operators was handed over to Panchayati Raj Institutions (PRIs). Due to poor
operation of STWs by PRIs, the Government decided to take over their
operation in July 2005.

ID again started construction of STWs of one cusec capacity in 2004-05. Of
the total irrigation potential of 34.15 lakh hectare created (as of March 2011),
1.66 lakh hectare was created during 2006-11 through 3521' new STW
projects with an investment of हैं 576.23 crore. Further, ID restored 1.85 lakh
hectare of Culturable Command Area (CCA) through re-boring of wells in
case of 1230 failed STWs (₹ 147.24 crore), improvement works of € 187.89
crore on defunct channels of 8350 STWs and replacement of pump sets of
5750 STWs.

2.1.2 Organisational setup

At the Government level, the Principal Secretary, ID is responsible for overall
monitoring and implementation of various schemes of construction,
modernisation and maintenance of STWs. Engineer-in-Chief, Mechanical
(E-in-C), assisted by Chief Engineer (CE), level-I (Tube well) and CE, Central
Equipment and Stores Procurement Organisation (CESPO) at the Department
level and four Zonal CEs and 23 Superintending Engineers (SEs) in the field.
Of the total 92 divisions, three Investigation and Planning Divisions,
four Procurement Divisions, 11 Construction Divisions, 70 Maintenance
Divisions and four Workshop Divisions are responsible forplanning,
procurement of materials, construction, modernisation/maintenance/operation
and manufacturing accessories for STWs respectively.

2.1.3 Audit objectives
The objectives of this performance audit were to ascertain whether:
e planning of the schemes was proper;

e funds were adequate, released in time and utilised economically,
efficiently and effectively;

e there was an effective mechanism in place for construction,
modernisation, renovation/reinstallation of tubewells and also
maintenance of already created assets;

' 234 STWs: Mukhya Mantri Ghoshana (Chief Minister's Declaration) and 3287 STWs: NABARD Funded.
eel

10


Chapter II : Performance Audit

e the schemes achieved the objectives to provide targeted irrigation
potential to the beneficiaries and whether the potential created was
utilised optimally and desired Benefit Cost Ratio (BCR) was achieved;
and

e the monitoring and evaluation mechanism was effective.
2.1.4 Audit criteria
Following were the sources of criteria adopted for the performance audit:

e Survey reports, preliminary plan proposals/detailed project reports of
selected projects and State Water Policy 1999;

e — Project guidelines, administrative and financial sanctions;
e = Annual work plan, Budget Manual and financial rules;

e — Circulars/instructions issued by the Government and Engineer-in-Chief;
and

e National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development's (NABARD’s)
terms and conditions and reports relating to release, repayment and
utilisation of loan.

2.1.5 Scope of audit

The performance audit covered the period from April 2006 to March 2011.
Records of 18 selected districts” were test checked during the period of audit.
The districts were selected applying Probability Proportionate to Size Without
Replacement method with stratified statistical sampling. Information was also
collected from the offices of the Principal Secretary, ID; E-in-C; Agriculture
Department; and NABARD. Joint physical inspection of 148 (10 per cent) out
of 1446 STWs, constructed/ renovated/modernised during 2006-11, was also
carried out together with interaction with the beneficiaries.

2.1.6 Audit Methodology

The audit objectives, criteria and scope were discussed with the Principal
Secretary, ID, Government of Uttar Pradesh (GoUP) and E-in-C in an entry
conference in 21 April 2011. The audit findings, conclusions and
recommendations were discussed in an exit conference in 16 January 2012
with the Principal Secretary of the Department and the replies of the
Department have been suitably incorporated.

2.1.7 Planning of the schemes

STWs are the major source for providing assured irrigation round the year
either in those areas where construction of gravity canals is not feasible or in
the areas which are not irrigated through canal. Tubewells are also used for

? Agra, Aligarh, Ambedkar Nagar, Banda, Bareilly, Budaun, Faizabad, Ghazipur, Gorakhpur, Hamirpur, Hardoi,
Jalaun, Kanpur, Lucknow, Mainpuri, Muzaffarnagar, Sant Ravidasnagar and Varanasi.

॥॥ 1


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
re el

conjunctive use of ground water in order to increase the intensity and
productivity of crops. Further, STWs require short gestation period and
provide quick irrigation facilities to areas under their command.

To help Schedule Cast/Schedule Tribe/Minority communities and marginal
farmers and also to create irrigation facility in unirrigated area (0.35 crore hectare)
of the State, GoUP sanctioned (February 2004) the scheme® for construction of
1000 new tubewells with schedule date of completion by 2006-07. Further, ID
also executed two other projects’ for restoration of already created irrigation
potential (1.88 lakh hectare) which could not be irrigated due to failure of STWs,
depletion of distribution system and wearing down of pump sets.

Audit examination of the three schemes revealed the shortcomings, discussed
in the succeeding paragraphs.

2.1.7.1 Planning without assessment of requirement

Construction of new STWs

ID started a pilot project, “Construction of 1000 Small State Tubewells”, in
2003-04 at a cost of % 81.91 crore in 57 districts, where sufficient ground
water was available for deep tubewells. The project was revised to हैं 98.19
crore for stated increase in cost of material, energisation charges and other
construction materials such as diesel, cement, bricks etc. The project was
completed in 2007-08 at हैं 98.22 crore. Similarly, ID sanctioned another
project for construction of 3000 new STWs at % 448.54 crore in 2007-08 with
completion schedule by 31 March 2010. The scheme was still in progress
(January 2012) and expenditure of % 444.52 crore had been incurred
(December 2011).

Scrutiny of the records revealed that the projects were formulated without
taking the requirements into account as no proposals regarding number and
location of STWs were obtained from the districts at the planning stage. After
the approval of the project by the Government, tubewells were distributed
among the districts on the basis of number of running tubewells and net
irrigated area. Subsequently, the concerned nodal tubewell division of the
district invited proposals from people's representatives, Gram Sabhas and
groups of farmers having a minimum of 50 hectare of unirrigated area.
Thereafter, the nodal division ascertained the feasibility/cligibility of the
construction of the STWs and submitted the proposals to District Tubewell
Site Selection Committee headed by District Magistrate for approval of
construction sites of the tubewells.

Further it was noticed that ID sent proposals (February 2009) to NABARD for
change of target of 25 unconstructed STWs (₹ 4.02° crore) in three districts

* Construction of 1000 new STWs.
“ Modemisation of failed STWs and improvement in distribution system and installation of new pump sets.
5 NABARD’s share: € 3,72 crore and State Share: & 0,30 crore.

due to saline water quality and absence of sufficient granular zone, which was
rejected by NABARD (March 2009). NABARD also requested (March 2009)
to ID to submit a proposal for cancellation of these project which was
forwarded to NABARD in May 2009.

The Government stated (January 2012) that the project was formulated
keeping in view the district-wise unirrigated area and stage of ground water
development but not on ad-hoc basis. Reply was not correct as the stated
criteria for selection of site and allocation of STWs were not adhered to, for
example, JP Nagar’, with more unirrigated area as well as higher possibility of
use of ground water, was allocated only 25 new STWs whereas Bagpat* with
less unirrigated area and less possibility of use of ground water, was allocated
75 new STWs.

Modernisation of failed STWs

The STWs failed after completing their useful life results in cessation of
irrigation facilities in their command area. To restore irrigation facilities in
such areas, modernisation of STWs was decided by re-boring of wells;
construction of new pump houses and head sumps; installation of new pump
sets; connecting existing distribution systems with new well points by laying
PVC pipe lines; renovation of existing distribution systems, if required; and
strengthening of field channels. Accordingly, proposals for re-construction of
800 out of 1227 such STWs, as on 1 April 2007, were prepared (July 2007)
excluding those where (i) water was saline; (ii) excessive number of private
tubewells were installed in the command of such STWs; (iii) STWs failed
before completing their economic life due to technical reasons; and (iv) there
were habitations now under the command of STWs. NABARD sanctioned the
project in October 2008 for RIDF loan.

However, non-availability of command area due to high concentration of
private tubewells in command area of originally proposed STW, ID sent
(September 2010) revised proposal to NABARD for modernisation of 470 out
of 800 such STWs. Further, during test check of the STWs in Ghazipur
district, it was noticed that after initial resistivity survey of these STWs,
ground water salinity was observed, hence 13 STWs of Ghazipur could not be
modernised.

While accepting the facts, the Government stated (January 2012) that due to
technical reasons, the modernisation of these 13 STWs was not taken up and
funds to the extent of € 1.91 crore were surrendered.

Overestimation of project

ID prepared a project for restoration of irrigation facility in 0.80 lakh hectare
command area during 2008-11 by modernising 800 failed STWs. The cost of
the project was to be met through loan from NABARD and State Government
in the ratio of 95:5. As per project report, the estimated cost of modernisation
of each STW was % 0.14 crore including the cost of survey, land, works,
equipment, contingency (two per cent), Special Tools and Plants, plantation,
charges for direction and administration and energisation. ID submitted
(September 2007) the proposal to Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC), for
evaluation of the cost of the project. On the recommendation of EFC, the
Government issued administrative and financial sanction of = 111.66 crore for
the project.

Scrutiny of the detailed project documents revealed that a provision of
& 2.03 crore (approximately हैं 25000 per STW for 800 STWs) was approved
for Special Tools and Plants for 800 STWs. However, in the proposal put up
for approval of EFC, provision for Special Tools and Plants was made at the
rate of = 45000 per STW. EFC approved the proposal of ID, which resulted in
excess approval and release of € 1.60 crore (¥ 20000 per STW for 800 STWs).
Thus, the failure of proper scrutiny of the project cost at E-in-C as well as at
the Government level, led to creation of liability of interest bearing loan of
% 1.60 crore extended by NABARD.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (January 2012) that the excess
amount released was utilised to cover the escalations in the cost of labour and
material. The reply was incorrect and also against the extant financial rules as
the funds should have been used for the purported purpose only. The excess
release of 1.60 crore should have been surrendered.

2.1.7.2 Viability of the project: Benefit Cost Ratio (BCR)

As per Paragraph 10 of Financial Handbook (FHB) Volume-VI read with
Appendix-IX of Financial Handbook Volume-V irrigation works have been
treated as commercial schemes. BCR is the ratio of annual benefits over
annual cost of the project. The annual benefit is the expected increase in
agricultural income, net of input cost, owing to the irrigation project. The
annual cost includes cost of operation and maintenance, depreciation and
interest burden on the project cost. Implicit assumption in its calculation is that
the running cost of the project would be made good as a result of increased.
earnings from higher production. If an irrigation project assumed BCR of one
or above one, it is considered commercially viable. Further, loans under RIDF
are also considered for sanction to those projects that have BCR>1. Audit
observed that:

Projection period

The project reports were based on unrealistic assumptions for calculating the
BCR. The projection period for BCR was taken as eight years while the capital
was to be invested within two/three years. Audit noticed (April-May 2011)
that Department calculated BCR for eight years instead for the period in which
the intended benefits have been achieved. Details are given in Table 4.

Table 4: Statement showing scheme-wise projection period taken for
calculation of BCR

Name of the project Projection BCratio Periodin Actual BC
period which Social _ ratio tal
(in years) Benefit benefit period

achieved 1.९., col. (5)

[1 | Construction of 1000 new STWs | 1:1.07 | 6 | 1:0.79
2 | Construction of 3000 new STWs | 8 | 1:1.24 [ 6 | 1:0.92
3_| Modernisation of 450 failed STWs | 8 | 1:1.64 [6 | 1:1.07

It is thus evident from the table above that the projects at SI. No. 1, 2, 4 and 5
were not commercially viable due to the BCR<1. Thus, the proposals for
sanction of projects were not based on actual data and were based on
unrealistic projections.

In reply the Government stated (January 2012) that guideline laid for the
formulation of project stipulates that after the probable date of completion of
project the revenue should be derived within a period of 10 years. Reply was

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

not acceptable as the intended social benefits of the projects were achieved
well before the period taken for calculation of BCR. Further, the BCR
projections for loan were unrealistic.

Envisaged cost benefit based on unrealistic running of STWs

Scrutiny of six project reports mentioned in the Table 4 above revealed that
the derived BCR was not based on realistic data. Social benefit was calculated
taking 1500 to 2500 hours of annual running of STWs, which was much more
than the actual average running of STWs during 2006-11 as given in Table 5.

Table 5: Statement showing year-wise running of STWs

It is evident from the above table that the actual running hours of STWs during
2006-11 ranged between 584 and 761 hours per STW. Scrutiny further
revealed that BCR was calculated on the basis of annual working expenses
which included (i) extension and improvement, (ii) maintenance of works and
(iii) energy charges.

The cost of annual working expenses were considered without accounting for

the cost of operation, though the expenditure on establishment was also to be
considered for assessing the BCR.

In reply (January 2012), the Government did not offer any comment to the
above observation.

project was to be reimbursed from borrowing the loan from NABARD at an
interest rate which was pegged at the rate of 0.5 per cent above the bank rate.
In addition, the Government also made budgetary provisions for specific!*
projects whereby the Government allotted funds to E-in-C who in turn
released it to EEs. Details of provisions made in annual budget and

the total created irrigation potential.

2.1.9.2 Non-energisation of STWs

The objective of various STWs schemes was to provide quick irrigation
facilities to the small and marginal farmers. To achieve the objectives of the
scheme, the constructed/modernised STWs were to be energised immediately
after the installation of the pump sets. Scrutiny revealed (August 2011) that
717 out of 2016 STWs constructed in the State during 2009-11, were not
energised till March 2011 despite transfer of हैं 13.12 crore to UPPCL for
energisation. Out of 717, 188 STWs (₹ 3.63 crore) were pertaining to the test
checked districts.

While accepting the Audit observation the Government stated (January 2012)
that out of 717 only 27 STWs were yet to be energised (December 2011) and
UPPCL was being regularly requested to expedite energisation. The reply was
not acceptable as Government did not furnish evidence in support of reply.

2.1.9.3 Construction of New State Tubewells

In 2006-07, ID prepared a project for construction of 3000 small STWs under
CCSY of one cusec capacity with 50 hectare CCA in 687 districts. These
STWs were proposed in each district. The energisation of STWs was entrusted
to UPPCL. Initially, NABARD sanctioned (November 2006) the project under
Rural Infrastructure Development Fund (RIDF)-XII at = 367.98 crore, which
was subsequently revised (November 2007) to = 448.54 crore (NABARD:
= 426.11 crore and State: = 22.43 crore). This project aimed at creation of
irrigation potential of 1.50 lakh hectare. The proposed work was to be
completed by March 2010.

Expenditure of € $76.23 crore was incurred on creation of additional 1.66 lakh hectare (34.15 lakh hectare - 32.49
lakh hectare) irrigation potential during 2006-11
3० Lalitpur and Sonbhadra districts excluded from the project duc to non-feasibility of construction.

The project envisaged construction of pump house, delivery tank, header for
PVC pipeline, brick work regulator, pump unit with switch gear, fuse/isolating
switch, capacitor, automatic starter and an hour meter for each STW. The
project also envisaged the construction of distribution system in each STW
consisting of 0.6 km underground PVC pipeline with five outlets and 3 km of
field channels. The examination of the records relating to the project revealed
the following irregularities:

Improper selection of site for construction of new STWs

The Government procedure for selection of construction site for new STWs
(August 2007) provided for (i) priority to those places where ground water
level was deep and because of which construction of private tubewell was not
feasible, (ii) non-construction in areas coming under the command of canals,
(iii) non-construction of STWs where the command areas of the proposed
STWs were flood prone or fell under banjar-kankrili soil and were therefore
not fit for Rabi/sugarcane crop etc.

Scrutiny of the records in the test checked districts revealed that in
contravention to the site selection order, 39 STWs were constructed at a cost
of % 5.36 crore. This was also confirmed during joint physical inspection of 10
per cent STWs constructed during 2006-11. District-wise details are given
in Table 9.

The Government stated (January 2012) that there was no ban on construction
of STWs where private tubewells existed in the command of the proposed
STWs and that these sites were selected by District Tubewell Site Selection
Committee. Reply is not correct as the construction of STWs in 50 per cent
cases in command area having more than three private tubewells and in the
command of canals and flood prone area was against the Government
procedure for selection of construction site for new STWs.

Non-construction of field channels

As per sanctioned project report, the water from the delivery tank passes
through a regulator and then via header into pipe lines in two directions with
five outlets spread over the command area of the tubewell. From every outlet
field channels, totalling 3 km in length, were to be constructed to distribute
water to all the fields in the command area of the tubewell. The construction
cost of STW was & 14.08 lakh for the first year and at enhanced rate of five
per cent and 10 per cent for subsequent two years. The construction cost
included the cost of € 0.21 lakh for construction of field channels for first year,
% 0.22 lakh for second year and % 0.23 lakh for third year.

Scrutiny of the records (April-August 2011) revealed that 19 out of
23 divisions (798 out of 965 STWs) of 18 test checked districts did not
construct field channels costing = 1.77 crore (Appendix-2.1.1) which were
mandatorily and essentially required for distribution of water in the entire
command of the tubewell. E-in-C issued strict instructions (June 2010) to all
divisions for construction of field channels as per provisions contained in the
project. The photographs below, taken during April-August 2011 in few
locations visited by Audit, depict that the field channels were not constructed.

335 Bah, Agra 110 ZG, Ghazipur

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that field channels had been
constructed on most of the STWs and on those STWs where the work of
construction of field channels was not taken up due to various reasons, were
being constructed as per provision. The Government was, however, silent
about non-construction of field channels at locations pointed out in audit.

Thus, due to non-construction of field channels, the Department could not
optimise the intended benefit of construction of new STWs and the funds
allocated for the purpose were utilised for other activities of STWs.

2.1.9.4 Projects for improvement of STWs

To ensure proper utilisation of STWs’ potential, ID started (2008-09) a project
named “Improvement in system of 11000 State Tubewells”. The project aimed
at installing new pump sets and accessories in place of worn out pump sets,
which had completed their economic running life and to modernise the
depleted distribution system of STWs to restore irrigation in the command
area, The expected irrigation potential of 48950 hectare was to be restored at
% 158.17 crore.

Scrutiny of the records revealed that the objectives of the scheme to restore
irrigation in 48950 hectares were not achieved due to reasons discussed in
succeeding paragraphs.

Pumps installed in case of STWs with low specific yield

As per Standing Order 1 of 1997-98 of ID, specific yield of the tubewell on
which pump set was being replaced should not be less than 1000 gallons per
hour per feet.

Scrutiny of the records of nine test checked TWDs (April-August 2011)
revealed that in contravention of the Standing Order, the pump sets were
installed on 55 STWs by spending हैं 1.15 crore which had yields of less than
1000 gallons per hour per feet. Scrutiny further revealed that the required
approval of SE for this deviation was not obtained before the installation of
the pump sets. Thus, the irrigation potential of the tubewells was not restored
as no significant increase in discharge was noticed even after the installation
of new pump sets. Details are given in Appendix-2.1.2.

On being pointed out in Audit, EE of the concerned TWDs stated that the new
pumps were installed on the demand of the farmers.

In reply, the Government, however, stated (January 2012) that it was
misrepresentation of information at the divisions’ level. Further, Government
also stated that on re-verification, it was found that there is not a single STWs
on which the specific yields is less than 1000 gallons per hour per feet and
significant increase in discharge has been found. The reply is not acceptable as

25


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

even from the revised details given by the Government, it is evident that in 33
out of 55 STWs, the discharge before replacement of the pump sets was below
1000 gallons per hour per feet. Besides, Government also did not furnish the
history chart of the STWs (original document) in support of their reply which
indicates actual yield of the STWs.

Thus, installation of pump sets on the STWs, which had insufficient water
strata, resulted in unfruitful expenditure of = 1.15 crore.

Pump sets installed on STWs with more than 70 per cent discharge

As per Standing Order 1 of 1997-98 of ID, pump set should be replaced only
when the discharge of the STWs is less than 70 per cent of the designed
discharge (33655 gallon per hour). However, 145 out of 306 pump sets,
replaced in seven test checked TWDs, and had discharge of water of more
than 70 per cent. Scrutiny further revealed that even after installation of new
pump sets in most of STWs, there was no improvement in irrigation potential.
Details are given in Appendix-2.1.3.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that pump sets which were
replaced, had completed their economical life and were worn out, required
frequent repairs. Reply was not correct as criteria for replacement should be
efficiency and not the life of pump sets. Moreover, in case of deviation in the
Standing Order, prior approval of SE was not obtained.

Thus, expenditure of = 2.58 crore incurred on replacement of pump sets was
against the prescribed norms.

No significant change in discharge of STWs

The objective of replacement of pump set was to increase the irrigation
potential of the STWs. Scrutiny of the records of the test checked divisions
revealed that due to improper assessment, discharge of the STWs remained
more or less the same despite replacement of pump sets during 2009-10 at a
cost of = 0.82 crore. Details are given in Appendix-2.1.4.

Scrutiny revealed that in six TWDs, discharge of the 46 STWs (20 per cent)
out of 235 STWs, where pump sets were replaced, remained almost same.
This indicates that replacement of pump sets was not required as the discharge
remained almost unchanged.

On being pointed out in Audit, EE of the concerned TWDs stated (April-
August 2011) that due to field requirement and demand of people
representatives pump sets were installed on the STWs.

In reply, the Government however, stated (January 2012) that it was
misrepresentation of information at the division level. Government also
furnished the details showing the increase in the discharge of the STWs. The
information furnished, however, revealed very meagre increase in the
discharge of the STWs. Besides, Government also did not furnish the history

26


Chapter II : Performance Audit

chart of the STWs (original document) in support of their reply which
indicates actual discharge of the STWs.

Thus, despite incurring an expenditure of % 0.82 crore on replacement of new
pump sets, the discharge of STWs remained almost the same and could never
achieve the designed discharge of 33,655 gallons per hour in any of the STWs

replaced.

2.1.10.2. Delayed handing over of drilling and development of STWs

was responsible for compliance to the above instruction. The instruction also
entailed that responsibility of officers would be fixed for delayed handing
over.

Scrutiny of records of the test checked divisions revealed that there were
delays ranging from 1 to 29 months in handing over of new STWs to TWDs
from the date of its development as detailed in Table 13.

2.1.11.1 Shortage of manpower

Adequate manpower was required for optimum utilisation of benefits from
STWs. Running of newly constructed STWs under two projects viz.
construction of 1000 and 3000 new STWs required tubewell operators.

As per paragraph 3(VIJ) of Irrigation Manual of Orders, one tubewell operator
is required to be posted for each STW.

During course of performance audit (April-August 2011), it was noticed that
there was huge shortage in the cadre of tubewell operators (responsible for
operation” of STWs). Scrutiny of records of test checked Tubewell Divisions
(TWDs) revealed that during 2006-11, 1062 new STWs were constructed in
these districts. The status of tubewell operators and running STWs (newly
constructed as well as existing) in these TWDs during 2006-11 is given
in Table 14.

A. built-in-internal control system provides reasonable assurance to the
department about compliance with applicable norms and rules thus achieving
reliability of financial reporting and effectiveness and efficiency in
departmental functions. Scrutiny of records revealed the following:

2.1.12.1_Improper/non maintenance of important records and registers

For effective control over financial and physical progress, E-in-C issued
(August 2007) instruction to EEs for proper upkeep of 21 prescribed records at
STW so that the inspecting authority can evaluate their performance. E-in-C
directed (December 2007) all EEs to maintain Works Register prescribed in
the Financial Hand Book Vol-VI.

Scrutiny of records revealed that Works Register, essential to watch the
progress of day to day expenditure incurred on a particular STW, were not
maintained in 18 test checked divisions. Due to non-maintenance of Works
Register, divisions failed to maintain tubewell-wise expenditure in Form 64 of
monthly accounts. This resulted in lack of control over allotment made for the
particular STW.

31


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
re el

Scrutiny further revealed that prescribed records, ranging from 10 to 217 were
not available at 53 out of 148 jointly inspected STWs. Further, the records
available were incomplete, unreliable and, therefore, did not serve the purpose.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that instruction had been
issued for keeping up all necessary records in the divisions as well as STWs.

2.1.12.2 Non closure of stock account

Paragraph 768 of FHB, Volume VI, provides the time schedule for six
monthly closing of stock account (September by 1 December and March by 1
August). In this regard, E-in-C also issued (December 2002) detailed
instructions for settlement of discrepancy noticed during verification and for
closing of stock accounts.

Scrutiny of records of the test checked divisions revealed that the stock
accounts were not closed from March 2006 to September 2010. This was
indicative of lack of internal controls in the Department. Details are given in
Appendix-2.1.5.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that instructions have been
issued to all CEs for closing of stock accounts by March 2012. Thus, due to
non-closure of stock account, stated stocks valued at = 73.76 crore were could
not be verified in audit.

2.1.12.3 Non-disposal of surplus/unserviceable items

The Government issued (February 2011) instruction for withdrawal of orders
for settlement of unserviceable items of the Department through Small Scale
Industries and instructed that settlement hereinafter be done as per procedure
contained in Appendix-XIXD of FHB-V.

Scrutiny of the records of CESPO revealed that 745.23 MT of worn out super
enamelled copper wire, PVC coated copper wire and steel/cast iron/aluminium
scrap (% 7.05 crore) and 209364 unserviceable machines/stores (€ 7.37 crore)
were lying in the divisions as of March 2011. Details are given
in Appendix-2.1.6. Possibilities of further deterioration and pilferage of these
stores cannot be ruled out.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that ID made efforts for the
disposal of items at the Central level but despite wide publicity, highest biding
prices of various items were below the norm. Reply was not acceptable as the
ID should have taken effective steps to dispose of the unserviceable items to
avoid further deterioration and pilferage of surplus/unserviceable items.
Further, this also resulted in blockade of = 14.42 crore.

Chapter II : Performance Audit

On the direction of State Government, E-in-C fixed (July 2007) norms for
monitoring/inspection at each level for proper functioning of STWs. CEs and
SEs would thoroughly inspect the STWs for a minimum of three/five days
respectively in a week and ensure immediate corrective action, in consultation
with the farmers. EEs were required to be available in the office till 12 Noon,
and after that they were to inspect the STWs and ensure immediate corrective
action, whenever needed, in consultation with the farmers.

Scrutiny of the records of the test checked divisions revealed that norms for
monitoring/inspection were not adhered to at any level. Only 10 to
24 monitoring meetings were held by the SEs in respect of 19 out of 23 test
checked divisions during 2008-11. Similarly, SE was required to inspect each
division under his control at least once a year. Scrutiny of 18 out of
23 divisions revealed that no inspection was carried out by SEs in eight
divisions during 2006-11. In eight, out of remaining 10 divisions, only one to
four inspections were carried out by SEs during 2006-11.

As the monitoring/inspection systems were not adhered to, ID failed to initiate
any remedial measures for improving the implementation of schemes,
maintenance of important records and consequently, the lessons learnt over a
period of time in implementing the schemes failed to bring about any systemic
improvements. ID did not evaluate the performance of the schemes to ensure
that the irrigation potential created was fully utilised.

In reply, the Government stated (January 2012) that the referred order was
issued under the prevailing drought conditions in the State, in special
circumstances. The routine work of monitoring and supervision was being
done at different levels as per norms and orders issued from time to time.
Reply was not correct as if the order ibid was for special circumstances that
would have been withdrawn and fresh order should have been issued.

The Department failed to achieve its objectives as the projects were not
formulated on ground realities and STWs were identified in an ad-hoc manner.
The projects were made deliberately economically viable as assumptions made
were based on unrealistic data. The Department could not utilise 73 per cent of
the created irrigation potential due to inadequate implementation of the
construction, modernisation and maintenance schemes. Further, payment for
energy charges was based on fixed rate of BHP, whereas, actual running of
STWs was less than the BHP verified resulting in creation of huge liability of
% 177.06 crore on account of electricity consumption charges. Adequate
manpower required for functioning of the newly constructed STWs was not
ensured. Internal control mechanism of the Department was also inadequate.

33

Chapter II : Performance Audit

IRRIGATION DEPARTMENT

Executive Summary

Uttar Pradesh Irrigation Department (UPID) initiated (April 2001) a
computer based Management Information System (MIS); supporting
administrative and water resource database and analytical & communication
facilities. MIS plan envisaged making computer based MIS operational in 48
months and taking over the system and withdrawal of consultant in next 6
months starting from April 2001. Even after expiry of 10 years computer
based MIS was a non starter in the Department due to deficiencies in project
implementation and also in system developed.

Major audit findings are highlighted below:

e Uploading of master and historical data was not yet completed.
Consequently not a single module has been put to use.

e The Department failed to provide prescribed training to the end users for
their skill development and effective use of the Application Software (AS).

e Department had not appointed the required technical experts for
implementation and maintenance of MIS.

e Slow and unreliable internet connectivity was a great stumbling block in
implementation of web based Application Software. Further there was
delay in delivery of service due to multiple vendors.

* Access control was inadequate. Administrative right of database was still
with the vendors.

e In the Application Software there was lack of data integrity and
consistency checks.

° Defective screen design of Application Software hindered smooth
operation, online help facility was neither user friendly nor topic specific.

e There was no disaster recovery plan.

As provided in Irrigation Manual prepared under the provisions of Northern
India Canal & Drainage Act-1873, Irrigation Department (ID) of the
Government of Uttar Pradesh (GoUP) is entrusted with the responsibility of
utilisation of water resources of the State for irrigation purposes by means of
canals, tube wells, dams & bundhies and of flood control activities. In April
2001, ID initiated, the UP Water Sector Restructuring Project (UPWSRP).

35


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Business process re-engineering part of UPWSRP provided for establishing a
Management Information System (MIS); supporting administrative and water
resources database; and analytical and communication facilities to modernise
and streamline Uttar Pradesh Irrigation Department business processes.
UPWSRP was funded by International Development Association (IDA), an
arm of World Bank. ID created a Project Activity Core Team (PACT)
comprising one Chief Engineer, one Technical Advisor and experts from the
fields of agriculture, ground water, rehabilitation, environment, geographical
information system, participatory irrigation management, procurement, legal,
finance etc. under a chairman to monitor the implementation of UPWSRP in
May 2002. Implementation schedule and financing plan of computer based
MIS were as under-

Table 1: Schedule for Completion of task and Financial Plan

(3) Implementation of MIS Project 24 months

(Source: UPWSRP Project Implementation Plan)

ID envisaged following benefits from computer based Management
Information System:

e Effective analyses and planning by using timely, accurate and reliable,
online available data and trends, for improved performance and early
detection of variations to effect speedy correctives;

e Increased efficiencies in routine tasks by online sharing and quick
retrieval of data, reduction in manual documentation and compilation; and

e Speedy decision making on real time data and trends available on state
wide network of computers.

Principal Secretary, Irrigation Department at the Government level and
Engineer-in-Chief at the Department level monitor the project. One post of
Chief Engineer (Information System) was created in the office of the
Engineer-in-Chief. To implement and co-ordinate MIS activities, a MIS Cell
comprising one Superintending Engineer (MIS Expert), two Executive
Engineers, two Assistant Engineers and two Junior Engineers were in place at


Chapter II : Performance Audit

the office of PACT. MIS Cell functioned under the supervision of Chief
Engineer, UPWSRP.

The Information Systems Audit was conducted to assess whether:

© user requirements were assessed properly and incorporated fully and
accurately in the Application Software;

accuracy, confidentiality, completeness, up-to-date status, reliability,
availability, timeliness and effectiveness of data were not compromised;

the MIS developed was being used for speedy and informed decision
making;

information Systems Assets and resources have been acquired as per
requirement, properly safeguarded and operated/used by trained
personnel; and

Monitoring and evaluation of the MIS was being carried out effectively.

Following were the sources of criteria adopted for Information System Audit:
e Project Document;

e User Requirement Specifications;

Financial Rules;

Documents relating to the targets and coverage of various schemes of
computerisation;

Best practices relating to IT controls; and

World Bank guidelines on selection of consultants/vendors.

2.2.7.1 Development of MIS Plan, Application Software (AS) and
acquisition of MIS & AS Assets

In order to facilitate timely decision by making comprehensive and relevant
database available on real time basis and at one place, the computer based MIS
was planned to be implemented by November 2007. Procurement of hardware;
development of software and procurement of consultancy services are
discussed in subsequent paragraphs. The selection of consultant/vendors was
based on Quality-cum-Cost based selection procedure prescribed in World
Bank guidelines.

Development of MIS Strategy

Although implementation of UPWSRP commenced from April 2001 and the
phase of “development of MIS strategy” was to be completed within four
months, the implementing agency, viz. PACT, was constituted in May 2002,
after a delay of more than one year.

MIS Plan, software development, implementation of MIS and networking

An agreement was executed (May 2002) with M/s Tata Consultancy Services
(TCS) to help and develop a MIS plan within eight months, and implement it
in the following 24 months; guide project operation, supervise staff training &
support in the following 12 months; and hand over the MIS systems in the
following six months at हैं 3.40 crore. The IS strategy consisted of (i)
Executive functions® (ii) Technical functions® and (iii) Support function’. M/s
TCS submitted report consisting of comprehensive User Requirement
Specification and MIS Plan, scheduled for submission within eight months
(January 2003), in June 2003 with a delay of four months.

Accounting, audit and draft para management, contract management, human resource management, payroll
accounting, litigation management, stores accounting, revenue collection, budget, procurement and tender
management, assembly & parliamentary questions and their reply management and asset management,

Inigation and water management, drainage improvement and maintenance, flood control and management,
investigation and planning, design of works, construction of projects, mechanical works, dam safety monitoring,
monitoring and evaluation

" Messaging and work flow system, document management system, information technology management system,

Chapter II : Performance Audit

Engagement of networking vendors

Although the networking vendor was to be engaged immediately after
submission of the MIS plan, PACT engaged (August 2004) M/s HCL Comnet
Limited for establishing state-wide, Wide Area Network (WAN) at
₹ू 15.79 crore, by April 2006. The vendor was also responsible for
maintenance of WAN and meeting bandwidth charges for six years, including
three years’ warranty, beginning April 2006. Thus, engaging the networking
vendor in August 2004, instead of immediately after submission of the MIS
plan (June 2003), resulted in delay by 14 months. The implementation of MIS
was further delayed as the vendor (M/s HCL Comnet) completed the work
with a delay of four years (April 2010).

The Government attributed (January 2012) the delay in award of the work to
procedural delays. Reply was not acceptable as a dedicated facility (PACT)
was created for designing and implementation of the MIS plan and the delays
indicated its failure in discharging duties. The Government also attributed the
delay in implementation of the network to non-availability of leased line
facility at various locations. The reply was not acceptable as the availability of
leased line facility should have been ensured before awarding the work.

The work of implementation of Local Area Network (LAN), in all the
555 offices, was to be commenced immediately after finalisation of the MIS
plan (June 2003). PACT sent the draft bidding document to World Bank for
approval on 1 May 2004, and World Bank issued No-objection certificate on
7 July 2004. However, the work was awarded in September 2005 to M/s GTL
Limited, at = 4.90 crore, with date of completion by August 2006. The vendor,
who was also responsible for hand-holding and maintenance of the system for
six years (including three years warranty, beginning August 2006) completed
the work in July 2007 with a delay of 11 months. Thus, there was delay of
approximately one year in sending the bidding documents to the World Bank
and a further delay of more than a year in the award of work.

The Government stated (January 2012) that the delay in implementation of
LAN was due to delay in finalisation of locations and changes therein and also
due to establishment of new offices. The reply was not acceptable as the
various locations for establishing network should have been ensured well in
advance and failure in this regard indicated inadequate planning.

Procurement of hardware

PACT procured (October 2004) 1000 desktop computers, printers, UPSs and
software (OS and MS office) from M/s HCL Infosystems Limited (Firm) at
= 7.28 crore. These items were supplied between January and March 2005 and.
carried three years’ warranty. After the expiry of warranty period, € 1.26 crore
was to be paid to the firm for maintenance of the above mentioned items for
the following three years. However, M/s Mahindra British Telecom Limited

39


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

was engaged for software development in October 2005 and various modules
were delivered between September and November 2009. Thus, by the time
software modules were made available, warranty period of all the desktop
computers and one and a half years, out of three years of the maintenance
contract period, had expired. This resulted in infructuous expenditure of = 1.89
crore on warranty and maintenance charges without use of hardware. This was
avoidable, had procurement of hardware been dovetailed with the delivery of
the software modules.

Government replied (January 2012) that hardware items were purchased in
advance and the staff was being provided basic computer training on it. The
reply was not acceptable as these computers, printers and UPS were for
implementation of AS and not for providing basic computer training.

Development of application software

Although the MIS plan prepared by M/s TCS was received in June 2003, [ID
engaged (October 2005) M/s Mahindra British Telecom Limited (now M/s
Tech Mahindra), after a lapse of 27 months, for development and submission
of AS, at = 12.90 crore. This included development and supply of application
system, general purpose software, training, conversion and migration of data
and six years of post implementation support. The vendor completed the
assignment in November 2009, with a delay of 25 months. Thus, there was a
delay of 27 months in selection of vendors/award of works and a further delay
of 25 months in delivery of the AS. The deficiencies noticed in the AS are
discussed in paragraph 2.2.7.2 below.

Audit observed that though the consultant (M/s TCS) submitted its report in
June 2003, there was delay in sending (May 2004) the draft bidding document
to World Bank for approval, and after receiving clearance from the later in
October 2004, the Department finalised the engagement of Software Vendor
in October 2005. Vendor had also delayed the completion of work by two
years, resulting in a total delay of four years in development of Application
Software.

Government replied (January 2012) that process for engaging software vendor
was started in February 2004 and there was procedural delay. Regarding delay
in completion of work Government stated that it was due to shifting of data
centre twice and also due to contract with vendor remaining ineffective
between 25 Feb 2008 to 06 June 2008. Reply was not acceptable as procedural
delays and shifting of data centre indicates lapse in planning the work.

Supply and installation of server

It was necessary to procure and install the server and associated hardware
immediately after receipt of the AS by the concerned vendor
(November 2007). However, ID took 22 months in placing the supply order

(August 2009) on M/s Vayam Technologies Limited for supply of a server,

Thus, procurements were not made in accordance with the implementation
schedule detailed in the MIS Plan of UPWSRP. For implementation of any
MIS synergies and synchronisation in procurement and commissioning are a
prerequisite. The development of computer based MIS in the Department was
replete with instances of advance procurements, delayed procurements,
delayed installations and commissioning etc. in key items/activities leading to
inordinate delays. MIS was a non-starter in the Department till date (January
2012) although more than 74 months have already been lapsed since its
scheduled date of completion (October 2005).

The Government stated that ID is using mailing and messaging services and
efforts are being made for implementation of MIS. However, these services
were just spin off benefits.

2.2.7.2 Implementation of Application Software
Screen Design and inbuilt help facility

Screen should be designed so as to ensure smooth operation and reduction in
likely input errors. Online help facility should be built into the AS to get topic
specific help easily. Audit observed that the “Opening screen’ of the AS was
congested (screenshot of opening screen is given below). Multiple windows
popped-up on the screen while entering data and the data were to be retrieved
from another table provided in look-up screens. This problem is there in every
module. Therefore, to facilitate easy data entry, drop down list boxes should
have been built-in. The Government stated that lookup screen was introduced
due to high volume of data. The reply was not acceptable as volume of data
does not come in the way of drop down list.

Online 'Help facility through use of function keys' was to be provided in AS.
This had been provided by loading module-wise user manual in MS Word
format. Each manual consists of 100 to 160 pages and was to be downloaded
for use. The access to help facility was neither topic specific nor user friendly.

Access Control

Users identify themselves to an ‘access control system’ by their usernames
and passwords. Therefore, to prevent misuse, the password should have a
periodic validity and after a pre-determined number of unsuccessful log-in
attempts the user profile should automatically get disabled.

Audit observed that:

न there was no pre-defined policy for password creation in terms of
number of characters viz. number of numerals, alphabets or a
combination of both;

. nine® out of 23 test checked offices had not received user name and
password;

e in Irrigation Division, Sultanpur the password assigned to the Executive
Engineer was not changed since its receipt from PACT (November
2010) and it was being used by all data entry operators;

e no restriction was built into the software against the number of
unsuccessful/unauthorised attempts and consequent disabling of the
password/locking of the system was not programmed.

The inadequate access controls were a threat to data integrity and increased
vulnerability and unauthorised access.

The Government stated (January 2012) that the password policy had not been
implemented keeping in view the user convenience and it would be
implemented soon. The reply indicated that data security was compromised in
exchange of convenience which was dangerous and risk ridden.

2.2.7.3 Delivery and Support
Delay in making current data available to the software developer

The developed Application Software, having 26 modules, covered the
executive, technical and support functions of the Department. For effective use
of the AS, it was necessary to upload and lock the historical and master data
before its implementation. M/s Tech Mahindra was to upload the master and
historical data. According to para 2.1.1 of the agreement and the clarification
of vendor of (July 2005), the vendor was to upload 23 GB historical/master
data at = 1.5 crore which were to be made available by the Department in time.
Though software vendor had handed over all the 26 modules to the
Department between September to November 2009, the master & historical

Inadequate training to staff

According to para 2.4.2 of the agreement, the vendor was to provide training
to at least two users per office per module. For the said training, a sum of
= 0.18 crore was paid to vendor in two installments (March and
October 2009). Details of training imparted, furnished to Audit by PACT did
not mention the modules for which training was provided. The examination of
available details revealed that 1867 out of 2566 trained persons received only
one day training and 374 persons were provided two days’ training against the
prescribed 10 days’. In 10 out of 23 offices, from where information was
collected, not even a single person was trained and in the remaining 13 offices,
employees were trained for use of only two to three modules. Training in the
area of database administration, helpdesk, application system support,
operation and facilities and management were provided only to private IT
persons hired for the project period.

Thus, the Department failed to provide prescribed training to the end users for
their skill development and effective use of the AS available. Further, there
was lack of technical capacity building for taking over the job after support
phase was over.

The Government stated (January 2012) that 10 days’ training in 40 batches
were provided to employees in operating all the 26 modules. As already stated
above, ID did not have details regarding modules in which various trainees
were trained and duration of training. However, training database and
information received from field offices did not support the Governments’
assertion.

Staffing

According to para 9.2.4 of the MIS Plan, successful implementation,
subsequent operation and maintenance of MIS necessitated that data and
information was managed as 'resource' of the Department as opposed to being
"owned" by the users. The high level interactions and dependencies between
computers, communications, office automations and technologies make it
imperative that these resources are managed as single organizational function.

The MIS Plan proposed a Departmental Information System Structure to
support the implementation of MIS technical plan, ongoing maintenance and
enhancement thereof. This proposal included appointment of Chief

44


In the Application

was lack of data

consistency
checks.

Chapter II : Performance Audit

Information Officer for overall responsibility for MIS; and System Managers
for application system support; database administration; network management;
mailing and messaging; Computer Aided Design (CAD); and Geographical
Information System (GIS) at State level. All these positions were to be held by
personnel technically qualified in the field of their assignment.

Scrutiny of records in PACT revealed that the Department (September 2004)
created one post of Chief Engineer (IS) as ‘Information System Officer’ at the
State level with overall responsibility for implementation and maintenance of
MIS. Posts of System Managers were, however, not created till date (January
2012). Further, the Department proposed (March 2009) to the Government for
posting of one Chief Engineer Level-2 as Chief Information System Engineer.
Posting of Superintending Engineer and Executive Engineer level officers of
defunct circle/divisions, as Senior System Engineers and System Engineers,
was also proposed. The proposal was, however, pending with the Government
(January 2012). In the absence of technical experts for implementation and
maintenance of MIS the Department was dependent upon the
vendors/professionals of AS, Server, LAN and WAN for utilisation of the
MIS.

Thus, the Department failed to appoint the required technical experts for
implementation and maintenance of MIS. Further, the proposal of the
Department to adjust surplus Civil Engineers, who did not have any
experience in Information Technology, against the posts of system engineer
was against the advice of MIS Consultant who had advised that Chief
Information officer and system managers should have seven to nine years’ and
four to six years’ experience respectively in IT related field.

The Government in its reply stated (January 2012) that the action for creation
and filling of posts was in progress.

Absence of input controls

Data of selected modules, out of the data of all the modules were obtained
(June-July 2011) from PACT in delimited text format and analysed using MS
Access 2007 to verify their accuracy, confidentiality, completeness, updation
status, integrity and consistency. Results were as under:

Human Resource Management System Module

This module, included personnel record management, manpower estimation,
recruitment, training, leave, complaint, grievance management etc., and was
designed to help the Department in effective utilisation of human resources in
order to enhance organisational performance. Data entry in this module was
started without assigning unique code to each employee of the Department.
Audit observed the following:

45


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

. employee Number column of Employee Table exhibited characters (both

. in Employee Table 26382 records had been entered. Duplicity was
checked on combination of name and date of birth fields and it was

° year of birth had been shown as 2008 in 4528 records, 2010 in 68
records, 2011 in 87 records and 2020 in 31 records. Year of birth shown
ranged from 1992 to 6420 in 68 records.

disabling validation checks for speeding up data entry was not proper which
resulted in erroneous data being fed.

Litigation Management Module

This module was designed to provide easy flow of information to facilitate
timely action on pending court cases and compliance to court orders. Analysis
of data obtained from department in delimited text form revealed that:

. case description column, for mentioning description of the case, was left
blank in 7084, out of 9021 entries in Case Table.

Chapter II : Performance Audit

° case number column for mentioning case number was lefi blank in 111

established.

checks were not introduced in the system.

Assembly and Parliamentary Question Reply management system module

This module intended to help in keeping track of the questions raised by the
Hon'ble members of Parliament and State Assembly. Data analysis of this
module revealed that:

e the Nodal Officer /d column was filled with ‘zero’ in 225 records out of
972 records available in Questions Table;

e diary number for each question should be unique but same diary number
was repeated from two to 79 times in 19 cases;

° check of duplicity of question description, in combination with session-
id and name of Hon'ble member revealed that 61 questions were repeated
in Questions Table from two to four times;

न authority for entry in Question Table was available to Executive
