of Chief Engineer, PACT indicating that user privileges were not
defined;

° instead of the ‘time taken for furnishing replies’ to the questions in
Questions table, name of week-day was entered in 270 records out of
972 records, in remaining records this column was left blank; and

. in reply table, the column 'Reply' was meant for entering reply, but only
word 'replied' or 'replied by' has been entered in 75 records.

47


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Evidently, such data would be of no use in monitoring submission of replies to
Assembly and Parliamentary questions, and the purpose for which the module
was designed remained unachieved.

Government replied (January 2012) that errors in data were being removed
and checks were being introduced. The reply was not tenable as checks
should have been implemented before data entry.

Financial Accounting Module

This module was developed for maintaining various registers, cash books and
to generate various reports such as cash outlay report, imprest account report,
transfer entry book, schedule of remittances, schedule of works expenditure,
schedule of suspense/deposit. Analysis of data available in this module
revealed that:

e in Cash Book Table, which stored summarised position of cash books,
there were only 20 records. All the records appeared to be test data. In
none of the records, closing balance equaled opening balance plus total
cash receipts minus cash payments. None of the closing balances
equalled cash in chest plus temporary imprest plus permanent imprest. In
three records all the values were zero. It revealed that closing balance
was not being calculated by the system. Values were entered manually
and accepted by the system;

. in Cash Book table Cash book period end-date was less than cash book
period start-date in one record (Record number 9), closing of 30 June
2011 had been entered on 29 June 2011 in three records (Record number
18 to 20), and the same was accepted by the system. Three entries were
accepted by the system for the same Administrative unit and month;

न in Generic Bill Table there were 424 records which were fed only by two
offices. Bill amount minus deduction amount did not match with amount
approved/amount passed. In 16 records, total of deductions under various
heads of deduction did not match with total deducted amount in
11 records; and

° In Cheque Book table, there were only 10 records. Number of leaves
available in Cheque Book Column, did not match with details fed in
‘cheque number from!’ to 'cheque number to! columns in three records.

Above discrepancies indicated that data integrity and consistency checks were
absent in the module.

The Government replied (January 2012) that errors were due to test data and
checks were being introduced. The reply was not acceptable as checks should
have been implemented, and test data must have been removed before data
entry.

48


Chapter II : Performance Audit

Budget Module

This module was to facilitate users to make budget request, watch budget
sanction, re-appropriations, and surrender of unutilised budget. Various
reports generated in this module were to help in analysing fund information
and in preparation of budget for the next year. Analysis of ‘Account Head
Table’ revealed that incorrect account heads such as “4701C22424”,
“567867888787806" were entered. Account head description given was also
incomplete. Thus, no checks were built into and the system accepted any value
and even incomplete Account head description.

The Government replied (January 2012) that validation checks had been
established. The reply was not acceptable as validation checks should have
been introduced before implementation of software.

Irrigation and Water Management Module

This module was to help the Department in efficient operation of canals, to
ensure timely maintenance of canal system, efficiency and accuracy in
recording revenue receipts and to minimise grievances of farmers. Analysis of
the data revealed that:

e The data of main canals, branches, distributaries and minors were
entered in a single table named Canal Table. Coding system for each
category of canal and also for each canal had not been developed. As
such completeness and accuracy of data could not be ensured and in
absence of coding there was no double check;

न Parent Identification mapping in the Canal Table was also incorrect.
There was one entry “SARYU MAIN CANAL” which was shown as a
branch of “Sharda Sahayak Feeder Channel”. Another entry “Saryu
Main Canal” which was shown as branch of “Basti Branch”, while Saryu
Main Canal was a main canal;

न Bed Level Column was blank in 9940 records; in 9840 record FSL (Full
Supply Level) was blank, tail-column was blank in 3302 records and
head discharge column was blank in 10363 records. In many records,
columns such as benefitted villages, point off take etc. were also lying
blank; and

. Water Users Association’s (WUA) member identification was system
generated and there was no check of duplicate entries. Duplicity was
checked on combination of WUA identification, name, address and
236 names were found repeated from two to 1088 times.

As such incomplete and inaccurate data entered in the system rendered the

module unusable.

The Government replied (January 2012) that errors were there due to presence

of test data which was being removed and coding system had been established.

49


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Reply was not acceptable as introducing coding system after entering a large
volume of data is of no use.

Mechanical Works Module

The key objectives of the module was to maintain details of all the tubewells
and minor lift canals and to keep track of all the construction works,
complaints and maintenance works relating to tubewells and minor lift canals.
In the tubewells Table, meant for storing details of all tubewells, there were
24234 records. Duplicity check on combination of circle offices, divisions and
departmental tubewell number revealed that same tubewell had been entered
twice in 383 cases, thrice in 13 cases and four times in one case indicating
absence of input control and haphazard data feeding.

The Government replied (January 2012) that validation system existed and
more checks were being introduced. The reply was not acceptable as all the
checks must have been introduced before starting data entry.

Flood Control and Management Module

For arranging relief work during floods, complete and accurate information of
floods and rainfall pattern is needed to assist the concerned authorities. This
module was to help in notifying the details of flood related information to
appropriate authorities of Civil Administration and the Department for
ensuring timely counter safety and relief measures. Analysis of the data
available in the module revealed that there were 156 records in Flood Forecast
Site table. In 74 records, the column, Danger Level and Highest Flood level
were blank; in Flood Forecast Reading table, all the records were entered by
temporary users. Flood Forecast Time column providing a time-stamp and the
year in the timestamp ranged from 1955 to 2003, revealing entry of non-real
life data and non-use of the module for the purpose for which it was designed.

The Government stated (January 2012) that instructions had been issued to all
the offices to check the data and remove test data and duplicate entries, make
necessary corrections and to update the data. The Government also stated that
validation checks were being introduced.

Multiple vendors

LANs established in each office, were integrated thorough WAN. M/S GTL
Limited established LAN in field offices and M/S HCL Comnet Limited
implemented WAN. In case of any problem in internet connectivity, users
were not aware as to which vendor required to be contacted for solution due to
unawareness of the exact nature of the problem. Multiplicity of vendors for
similar nature of work, led to delays in smooth delivery of services.

The Government stated (January 2012) that separate vendors were engaged for
domain expertise and efforts were being made for early disposal of
complaints.

50


There was no
capacity building
in the Department
and even for
routine tasks
Department was

vendors.

Chapter II : Performance Audit

Dependence on vendors

Vendors for software development and maintaining servers still held the
Administrative rights of database. Consequently, the Department was
dependent on these vendors for routine tasks like deletion of any record from
backend, taking up backup of database etc. Sometimes there might be need for
backend corrections, though backend corrections should not be permitted in
normal course, but as there was lack of validation checks as already
mentioned, in para relating to input controls, for which Department was
dependent on vendors. Provision of backend access to vendors was a risk to
data integrity.

The Government stated (January 2012) that administrative rights were in
control of MIS Cell. The reply was not correct as despite passage of
considerable time, and the vendor support period nearing to end, the
Department had not buildup adequate capacity to take over the system after
support phase is over.

Slow and unreliable internet connectivity

Like any other web-based application, internet service was necessary for use
of the AS. The internet speed should also be fairly good for quick response
time and without broken working rhythm.

Internet connectivity was provided to users through LAN and WAN. The latter
was established through BSNL leased line. Expenditure on establishment of
LAN was % 4,90 crore ( 3.27 crore one time and % 1.63 crore recurring for
the period from August 2007 to July 2013, 100% of capital amount has been
paid and recurring payment is being made). Expenditure on WAN was
= 15.79 crore (₹ 0.84 crore one-time and % 14.95 crore for the period from
May 2010 to April 2016). At each LAN site, internet speed provided (upto 64
Kilo Bytes per Second) were not adequate to access AS and enter data. In test
checked offices, it was observed that on opening the departmental web-site it
takes a long time. Sometimes, repeated attempts were required to log-in. Once
logged in, the internet got disconnected automatically. This was a great
stumbling block in the implementation of computer based MIS. Ten out of
twenty three test checked offices, confirmed inadequate connectivity and nine
offices stated that there was no internet connectivity.

Thus, even after incurring such a huge amount on LAN and WAN
(₹ 4.90 crore on LAN and % 15.79 crore on WAN), the Department started
distributing USB modems to each office for accessing internet. Two out of 14
test checked offices, had even taken BSNL broadband connection which
resulted in wasteful expenditure on LAN and WAN of % 20.69 crore’ and

® % 20.69 crore (LAN -₹ 4.90 crore + WAN - ₹ 15.79 crore)

51

no disaster

extra expenditure of % 6.59 lakh'® on procurement of USB modem and

The Government stated (January 2012) that there was a proposal to increase
bandwidth upto 10 MBPS in the second phase of the project (UPWSRP). The
reply indicated that the data load and the need of band-width for use of the AS
was not assessed at the time of planning and implementation of the computer
based MIS.

Disaster Recovery and Business Continuity Plan

There must be a plan in position to ensure that the Information System
continues to function even in the event of a failure of information processing
resource. For ensuring continuity of work, it was necessary that Database
Backup is kept in a safe place away from the data centre so that data remains
secure in case of any disaster. Further for computer based MIS of such a large
magnitude a disaster recovery site should be planned. The Department has not
documented any Disaster Recovery/Business Continuity Plan. In the
Department, there was no alternate data centre site and backup was being
stored at data centre itself exposing the Department to high risk of stoppage of
the MIS activity in case of destruction of data centre.

The Government stated (January 2012) that there was a proposal to develop a
Disaster Recovery System in the second phase of the project (UPWSRP). This
indicated that the MIS plan was faulty to the extent that it did not provide for a
disaster recovery and business continuity plan.

Vendors handed over all the modules of AS to the Department between March
and November 2009 but the Department did not make any plan for gradual
shifting from the manual system to computer based MIS. In the absence of
such a plan there was no yardstick against which progress of implementation
of computer based MIS could have been monitored.

The Government stated (January 2012) that all the Chief Engineers were
instructed (January 2012) to get the MIS modules implemented. However, due
to lack of proper monitoring MIS modules were yet to be fully implemented.

Even after spending = 68.97 crore (January, 2012) and after expiry of about 10
years the progress of implementation of computer based MIS in the Irrigation
Department was far from satisfactory. The objective of effective planning and
analyses based on the factual data, increased monitoring, enhanced
transparency and communication could not be achieved due to non
preparedness of the Department in making current data available to the
vendor, poor internet connectivity and inadequate training. Till date

°° 6.59 lakh (₹ 3.84 lakh on USB modem + % 2.75 on recurring charges)


Chapter II : Performance Audit

(January 2012), not a single module, out of 26 modules, has been put to use,
though these modules were handed over to the Department, by the vendor,
between September and November 2009, besides the hardware procured in
October 2004, also became outdated. Till date, even master data had been fed
partially and that too were unreliable. Department also failed to protect the

system/data from any disaster.

Unique codes should be assigned to each officer/employee, canals,
Tubewells and minor lift canals for avoiding duplicity of data; input and
validation controls should be built-in to ensure completeness and
correctness of the data;

The backlog of data entry should be completed at the earliest for
ensuring availability of historical and master data and facilitating
expeditious decision process;

Data backup should be stored at a place away from the data centre; and
a disaster recovery site & business continuity plan should be put in
place;

Proper monitoring efforts should be made to make all the 26 modules
put to use and appropriate training should be imparted to staff; and

Issues of LAN/WAN and bandwidth needs to be addressed and resolved
with promptitude.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

JCATION DEPARTMENT

“UP Education for all Project Board” was constituted in May 1993 to manage
the World Bank assisted project and the powers to manage the primary and.
upper primary schools were vested with the “Village Education Committees
(VEC)”. The Board also manages the Centrally sponsored Sarva Shiksha
Abhiyan (SSA), 2001 which aims at ensuring availability of a well equipped,
well staffed, well functioning and well attended primary and upper primary
schools, providing useful and relevant elementary education cutting across all
social, regional and gender gaps, to all children in the age group of six to 14
years by the year 2010. The Right of Children to Free and Compulsory
Education Act, 2009 (RTE) envisions free and compulsory education to all
children in the age group of six to 14 years.

Keeping in view the aforesaid objectives, the State Government initiated
number of steps which included opening of new schools in un-served
habitations, adding new class rooms to existing schools, recruitment and
training of teachers to improve pupil-teacher-ratio and reorientation of the
teaching skills, effective implementation of SSA activities and Mid-Day Meal
(MDM) scheme etc.

Principal Secretary, Basic Education Department; State Project Director, SSA;
Director, Basic Education; Basic Shiksha Adhikari (BSA); and
Deputy/Assistant BSA and Village Education Committee at the Government,
department, district, block and school levels, respectively, were responsible
for implementation of the aforesaid programmes.

To evaluate the status of availability of infrastructure facilities and teaching
aids in the Government “Primary Schools” (PSs) and “Upper Primary
Schools” (UPSs) and its impact on basic education during 2006-11, Ghazipur
(GZP) and Gautam Buddha Nagar! (GBN) were selected on the basis of
geographical locations, economic conditions and inter-district disparity” in the
State. Table 1 exhibits the population, number of children (0-6 years), number
of PSs/UPSs in both the districts and sampled PSs/UPSs for detailed
examination.

2 Ghazipur lies in the State’s eastern border and Gautam Buddha Nagar in western border.

? ‘The Govemment has worked out the level of development in the state by Ranking Mcthod at district level on the
basis of 36 selected indicators under economic infrastructure, social infrastructure etc. On this basis, the districts
have been placed in five ranges of Composite Index of Development (CID): very high, high, medium, low and very
low. While Ghazipur is under very low CID with 71.90 points, Gautam Buddha Nagar is under very high CID with,
431.44 points.

2.3.6.2 Dropout and Retention of children

As per recommendations of SSA programme management the children
enrolled should continue to remain in school to complete their basic education.
Children leaving schools midway indicates either non-availability of good
educational environment/teachers or lack of proper atmosphere/ facilities.

In 463 PSs of GBN, 8161 children out of 12421 children enrolled in Class-IIT
(2008-09), took admission in Class-V (2010-11). Thus, 34 per cent children
dropped out and did not continue their education in the Government schools
during the period. Similarly, in 208 UPSs, 3421 children out of 4791 children
enrolled in Class-VI (2008-09) were admitted to Class-VIII (2010-11),
resulting in drop out of 29 per cent children at UPSs level during the period.
Information relating to GZP was not made available.

is shown in Table 10.

Universalisation of elementary education by providing sufficient number of
teachers, adequate infrastructure facilities like toilets, separate toilets for girls,
safe and adequate drinking water, mid-day-meal, sports grounds in the
Government Primary and Upper Primary Schools were not largely fulfilled in
the Ghazipur and Gautam Buddha Nagar districts of the State. Enrolment in
Government PSs/UPSs was on declining trends (44 per cent in GZP and 19
per cent in GBN during 2006-11 in class-1) and dropout in these schools was
also significant (47 and 37 per cent at the primary level respectively in GZP
and GBN) in the scenario of growing population of the State. Shortage of
teachers in the sampled schools, vis-a-vis, the overall position of both the
district also effected the achievement of objective of the scheme. The
Computers were provided to 10 UPSs of GZP, of which seven were not
installed due to non-availability of electric supply. Distribution of free text
books and uniform to all girls, SC/ST children at primary and upper primary
levels was not ensured in all Government PSs/UPSs. Besides, 59 per cent
sampled PSs and 81 per cent sampled UPSs of GZP and 20 per cent sampled
PSs and 29 per cent sampled UPSs of GBN lacking library facilities. The
transition rate of students from class-V to VI in sampled schools of GZP
ranged between 63 and 74 per cent and 52 and 60 per cent in GBN.

Despite availability of sufficient class-rooms, improved TSR, MDM and other
facilities, the Government was not able to restrict the drop out of the children
in the Government schools.

e Universalisation of elementary education should be ensured by reducing
drop out rate and bringing children into the education stream;

e The infrastructure facilities and teaching aids, provisioned in the
programmes launched by Gol and State, should be ensured to every
school;

e Supply of free text books and uniforms to all eligible children should be
ensured; and

e The shortage of teachers be mitigated in the schools by deployment of
teachers and proper training to the teaching staff should be provided for
better quality of education.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

MEDICAL HEAL ND
FAMILY WELFARE DEPAR

Internal
controls
were lax.

With a view to promoting good health and expand the outreach of health care,
Medical Health and Family Welfare Department (MHFWD) runs a network of
hospitals comprising District Hospitals (DHs), Community Health Centres
(CHCs), Primary Health Centres (PHCs) and Sub centres. In 1994, the
Government of Uttar Pradesh (GoUP) set norms for medical equipment and
apparatus for these hospitals. In 2006, the Government of India (Gol) also
prescribed health standards (designated as Indian Public Health Standards-
IPHS) defining the level of quality of the hospitals/centres should meet with
the key aim to enhancing the quality services delivered.

At the Government level, the Principal Secretary, assisted by the Director
General, Medical and Health Services, Lucknow (DGMHS) at the State level,
the Chief Medical Superintendents (CMSs) for DHs/ DJH and Chief Medical
Officers (CMOs) for CHCs, PHCs and Sub centres at district level were
responsible for the implementation of activities of MHFWD.

To evaluate the status of medical equipment and apparatus for 2006-11,
Ghazipur and Gautam Buddha Nagar were selected! on the basis of
geographical locations, economic conditions and inter-district disparity in the
State.

Records for 2006-11 were examined (May and June 2011) in the offices of
DGMHS, Lucknow, CMSs of DHs/ DJH and CMOs of Ghazipur and Gautam
Buddha Nagar. Besides, records of five CHC3? out of ten, 15 PHCs* out of 87
and 59 Sub centres" out of 521 were also examined. Results of examination are
discussed in succeeding paragraphs. These findings were discussed (January
2012) with the Principal Secretary, MHFWD. The results of discussions and the
replies of the Government have been suitably incorporated.

Audit findings

During 2006-11, DGMHS provided ₹ 178.96 crore to the CMOs and CMSs,
Ghazipur (% 137.49 crore) and Gautam Buddha Nagar (रे 41.47 crore) against

Ghazipur lies in State’s eastern border and Gautam Buddha Nagar in western border. The Government has worked.
out the level of development in the state by Ranking Method at district level on the basis of 36 selected indicators
under economic infrastructure, social infrastructure etc. On this basis, the districts have been placed in five ranges
of Composite Index of Development (CID): very high, high, medium, low and very low. While Ghazipur is under
very low CID with 71.90 points, Gautam Buddha Nagar is under very high CID with 431.44 points
Ghazipur: Khanpur, Muhammadabad and Saidpur; Gautam Buddha Nagar: Badalpur and Bhangel.

Ghazipur: Abhisan, Asawar, Barachaur, Betawar, Bhadaura, Karanda, Kusmikalan, Mardaha, Reotipur,
Subhakarpur, Zamania and Gautam Buddha Nagar: Barela, Bisrakh, Dankaur and Sector 22.
Gautam Buddha Nagar: 17 and Ghazipur: 42.

Chapter II : Performance Audit

which %166.67 crore (Ghazipur: = 128.45 crore; Gautam Buddha Nagar:
% 38.22 crore) was utilised in providing health care, leaving overall savings of
र₹ 12.29 crore (Ghazipur: = 9.04 crore; Gautam Buddha Nagar: & 3.25 crore).
The details of allotment, expenditure and savings are given in Table 1.

Scrutiny revealed (May-June 2011) that the funds were allotted for pay and
allowances on the basis of sanctioned strength of staff rather than person-in-
position in violation of Paragraph 32 of the Budget Manual’ resulting in
savings of हैं 12.29 crore. Scrutiny also revealed that CMO, Gautam Buddha
Nagar did not maintain the register for allotment and expenditure during
2006-11. As such the register was not submitted to the Controlling Officer,
depriving him of the opportunity to exercise expenditure control, as required
under Paragraphs 112 and 116 of the Budget Manual*. Further, CMO, Gautam
Buddha Nagar did not record day-to-day transactions (involving हैं 4.96 crore)
in cash book for the months of February and March 2011. The monthly
balances (involving = 16.24 crore) from June 2010 to January 2011 were also
not authenticated by DDO (as of June 2011).

The Government stated (January 2012) that the funds were allotted in
anticipation of appointment of doctors by the Public Service Commission. The
Government also stated (January 2012) that the CMO, Gautam Buddha Nagar
had since authenticated the cash book entries. The replies were not acceptable
as the fact remained that the statutory provisions (Rule 32 of Budget Manual)
were violated, resulting in savings of हैं 12.29 crore. No reply for non-
maintenance of register of allotment and expenditure was furnished
(March 2012).

Thus, internal controls were lax not only in allotment of funds but also in
utilization thereof which was fraught with the risk of misappropriation
/embezzlement.

Ultra sound machine

noticed that the patients had ultra sono graph reports of the private diagnostic
centres. Thus, the ultra sound machine remained idle, as of May 2011, even
after five years of its installation and the patients did not have any option but
to visit private diagnostic centres for ultra sono graphs all along.

X-ray machine

In DH (Male), Ghazipur, six X-ray machines (रे 13.41 lakh) were installed
prior to 2006-07. However, as of March 2011, three were unserviceable since

disposed of even after seven years due to inaction. Out of the remaining two,

one was in service and 7,405 x-rays were conducted during 2009-11, while

Cardiac equipment

The Government sanctioned cardiology department for DJH, Gautam Buddha
Nagar prior to 2006-07 and equipped it with computerised ECG, four cardiac
monitors and two cardiac monitors with defibrillator'’. The Government also
sanctioned (June 2005) a post of cardiologist for the department. However, the
equipment were not being used for treatment of patients and remained idle as
the cardiologist was not posted by DGMHS, as of June 2011.

The Government stated (January 2012) that cardiologist could not be posted
due to their non-availability.

Electrolyte Ion Analyser

AUDIT OF TRANSACTIONS

Audit of transactions of the Government departments, their field formations as
well as that of the autonomous bodies brought out several instances of lapses
in management of resources and failures in the observance of the norms of
propriety and economy. These have been presented in the succeeding
paragraphs under broad objective heads.

3.1 Non-compliance with rules and regulations

For sound financial administration and financial control it is essential that
expenditure conforms to financial rules, regulations and orders passed by the
competent authority. This not only prevents irregularities, misappropriation
and frauds, but helps in maintaining good financial discipline. Some of the
audit findings on non-compliance with rules and regulations are hereunder.

3.1.1 1,058 to Government

Authority without realising its full value led to a loss of = 2,632.19 crore.

As per the State Financial Rules' all Government land and immovable
property should ordinarily be sold through the Revenue Department.

Scrutiny of records of Executive Engineer (EE), Head Works Division, Agra
Nahar, Okhla revealed (March 2010) that the Government, instead of
transferring 317.63 acre spare land located in South Delhi* to New Okhla
Industrial Development Authority (Authority) through the Revenue
Department, decided (July 2008) to transfer the land directly on payment, for
development purposes and issued notification (October 2008). The
Government also directed (January 2009) to complete all the requisite legal
formalities including realisation of full cost of land at the prevailing circle rate
by the Irrigation Department before the registration and transfer of possession
of the land in favour of the Authority. Scrutiny also revealed that in addition to
the aforesaid land, the Principal Secretary, Irrigation Department also ordered
(January 2009) to transfer 184.292 acre* land located in Gautam Buddha
Nagar directly to the Authority.

The EE transferred’ and allowed possession of the land (501.922 acre) during
January 2009 to May 2009, to the Authority without recovering full value of

the land from the Authority as directed, and raised demand for % 150 crore as

remained that the land was transferred without realising full value.

3.1.2 Unlawful demolition/construction

Chhatrapati Sahuji Maharaj Medical University, Lucknow demolished
a heritage building in Lucknow and on the same site constructed a
new one at हैं 28.30 crore in disregard of the Ancient Monuments and
Archeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958,

The Ancient Monuments and Archaeological Sites and Remains Act, 1958 and
tules framed thereunder not only prohibit the demolition of the building which
was declared heritage by the Archaeological Survey of India (ASI) but also
mandated to obtain the “No Objection Certificate” from it prior to construction.
or re-construction or repair or renovation of such building or structures which
are located in prohibited and regulated areas.

Scrutiny of the records of Chhatrapati Sahuji Maharaj Medical University,
Lucknow revealed (January 2011) that the University owned a building which
was falling under the heritage zone’ declared (1920) by the ASI. The building
was also situated within the regulated area of the Conserved Monument of
Imam Bara Asaf-ud-daulah*, Lucknow in terms of the Act, ibid. However, the
University proposed to the Government (September 2007) for demolition of
heritage building and to construct a new building in its place at 29.11 crore.

The Government, in disregard of aforesaid rules and regulations, accorded
(March 2008) administrative approval and financial sanction to the proposal.
The works of demolition and construction were assigned to the Uttar Pradesh

and constructed a new one at हैं 28.30 crore (as of March 2011). Scrutiny also

of internal structure. Moreover, four other buildings were also under

Certificate” from the ASI. Thus, not only the demolition of the heritage
building but also the construction of a new one at हैं 28.30 crore did not
conform to the rules, regulation.

In reply, the University accepted (January 2011) the facts and stated that it
would be avoided in future. The reply was not acceptable as the fact remained
that the heritage building was demolished in violation of the extant Act and
tules. The Government accepted (November 2011) the facts.

3.1.3. Excess expenditure

An excess expenditure of = 2.02 crore was incurred by the Provincial
Division and Construction Division, Deoria in construction of cement
concrete roads in disregard to approved specifications and norms.

As per the detailed guidelines issued (July 2008) by the Government for
construction of Cement Concrete roads, covered drains under Dr. Ambedkar
Gram Sabha Vikas Yojna 2008-09, the administrative departments/executing
agencies were to adopt specifications scrupulously, consumption norms of
material as well as standard drawings and designs for execution of works.
The model estimate in the guidelines was based on standard designs and
drawings, consumption norms and the unit rates and was approved by the
State Planning Institute!’. However, a committee headed by the Principal
Secretary, Panchayati Raj was empowered to decide on policy matters and
Zila Panchayat Anushravan Koshthak (Government Level) was empowered.
to take decisions on technical matters under the guidelines. The executing
agencies were not competent to deviate’ change model estimate without prior
approval of the Government.

Scrutiny of the records of Executive Engineer (EE), Provincial Division and
EE, Construction Division, PWD, Deoria and further information collected
revealed (April 2010) that concerned EEs accorded technical sanction to
estimates for the works'! of Cement Concrete roads and covered drains by

3 Salvage value 14.69 lakh (as on 30 March 2008) after demolition.
2० Working as planning department of the Government
“1 Executive Engineer, Provincial Division, PWD, Deoria: 15 work and Construction Division, PWD, Deoria: 20 work.

83


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
—_ 34 94 आह 4991 3३3७3), ४३333.30 43.

making changes in their designs and consumption norms and thereby violated

the guidelines which resulted in an extra expenditure of = 2.02 crore as

discussed below:

e Under the scheme, Cement Concrete roads were to be constructed by
providing the base of lean concrete containing the composition of cement,
fine sand and brick ballast in the ratio of 1:6:12 respectively. In both the
divisions, the constituent of brick ballast in lean concrete in road works
was replaced by costlier stone ballast. Deviation from the approved
specifications led to excess expenditure of हैं 1.32 crore as detailed in
(Appendix-3.1).

e As per approved design and drawings, drains were to be covered with
RCC” cover of 60 cm width and 5 cm thickness and the consumption of
mild steel (iron work) in RCC cover was allowed at the rate of one
per cent of the cubical quantity’? of RCC cover. However, deviating from
these norms, provision for RCC drain cover was made for 7.5 cm
thickness in both the divisions. The increase in the thickness of the drain
cover led to excess expenditure of € 0.70 crore (including cost of the Mild
Steel) as detailed in (Appendix -3.2).

Thus, deviations from the approved specifications and consumption norms led
to an excess expenditure of € 2.02 crore.

In reply, the Chief Engineer (CE), PWD Headquarters, Lucknow stated
(August 2010) that Superintending Engineer (SE) was competent to change
the material and quantity as per condition of the site. The reply was not
acceptable as the cases of inevitable modifications of norms should have been
referred by the executing agencies to the Government with full justification, to
obtain permission for deviations from the norms.

While admitting the facts and figures, the Government, during discussion, stated
(November 2011) that works with deviation were executed due to site
conditions and to provide structural soundness. The reply is not acceptable as
model estimate was approved by the State Planning Institute and CE/SE was
not the competent authority to deviate/change the model specifications.

3.1.4 Violation of specifications

Non-adherence to specifications resulted in not only sub-standard
works worth हें 2.50 crore but also avoidable expenditure of ~ 2.82 crore
on renewal works of village roads.

As per the instruction issued (July 1998) by the Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C),
Public Work Department, Lucknow, all the construction works were to be
executed in accordance with the norms/specifications of Ministry of Road

* Reinforce cement concrete.
One cum of RCC work required 78.5 kg. mild steel.

84


Chapter III : Audit of Transactions
टिक

Transport and Highways (MORTH) and also of the Department. However, the
following deviations from the specifications were noticed in audit.

e In terms of the standard specifications (June 1974 and February 1984) of
the Department and also the specifications of MORTH, the soling coat of a
road pavement should be provided with stone ballast of appropriate size
(i.e. 45-90 mm). Bricks on the edge are not the approved material for use
in the soling coat because the crushing strength of brick (140 Kg/Square
centimeter) is less as compared to that of stone ballast (650 Kg/ Square
centimeter).

Scrutiny of records of Executive Engineer (EE), Provincial Division,
Balrampur revealed (May 2010) that the Government sanctioned (June 2008)
% 11.39 crore for the widening and strengthening of Utraulla-Faizabad-
Allahabad road (State Highway). The required crust in the widened portion
was achieved by laying old dismantled bricks on edge (BOE) in the soling
coat in place of stone ballast of appropriate size, as prescribed in the
specifications, in Kms 4, 7, 11, 15 and 17. Thus, the work in the widened
portion so executed was below to the specified norms as the strength was less
than that required to sustain the axle load of the commercial vehicles. Further,
the work of strengthening over a weak soling in the widened portion was also
bound to suffer adversely due to pavement distortion, rendering total works
costing हैं 2.50 crore substandard as detailed in Appendix 3.3.

In reply, the EE stated (May 2010) that it would be of sufficient strength as the
widened portion would remain outside of the main carriageway. The reply was
incorrect because the widened portion is also a part of the main carriageway.
This was also corroborated by Central Road Research Institute, New Delhi, by
stating that the use of bricks in soling coat would certainly affect the
performance of the pavement in the long run because the soling layer would
not have sufficient strength.

e In terms of the E-in-C’s circular (June 2007), the same material'* as was
used in second coat (P2) of two coat surface dressing (painting) should be
used for renewal of village roads.

Test check of records of the EE, Provincial Division, Sitapur revealed
(November 2010) that contrary to the aforesaid instruction, the renewal of 34
village roads {(Total 2,87,676 square meter area (sqm)}, was done with
premix carpeting with seal coat (PC/Seal coat) which was of higher
specifications. An expenditure of = 4.50 crore (Appendix 3.4) during 2009-10
instead of = 1.68 crore (admissible at the rate of € 58.50 per sqm) according to
P2 specification, was incurred. Thus, an expenditure amounting to ~ 2.82 crore
was incurred using higher and costlier specification in renewal work of village
roads, which otherwise could have been avoided.

14 80/100 Peneteration Grade Bitumen and 13mm nominal size pre-coated grit.
SS

85


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

The EE stated (November 2010) that renewal of village roads with PC/Seal
coat was done as per circular of E-in-C (August 2007). Justifying the PC/Seal
coat works, E-in-C also stated that damage to the surface of roads every year

was a regular feature as these were in flood prone area. The reply was
incorrect as the above circular was for construction of only new village roads
and was not applicable to the renewal works. Further, in the adjoining
(Lakhimpur Kheri) district of the same circle, Pz specification has been
adopted for renewal of village roads during the same period.

During discussion (November 2011), the Government while admitting the
facts and figures stated that by using old dismantled bricks the money was
saved without compromising the quality of work, in the former case. The reply
was not acceptable as quality of work could not be assured without adherence
of the prescribed specifications. However, no justification was furnished in
respect of the other case.

3.1.5 Extra payment to contractors

Extra payment of = 1.89 crore was made to contractors by the Chief
Engineer, Allahabad Zone due to non updation of Schedule of Rates.

As per State Financial Rules'®, Schedule of Rates'® (SoRs) for each circle
should be updated in April/ May each year after market surveys and taking
into account the prevailing rates, taxes and duties. The Superintending
Engineers are responsible for updation of the SoRs.

The Government revised (June 2004) the transit fee from % five to हैं 38 per
Metric Ton on timber wood and other forest produces including lime-stone,
‘laterite' rocks ete. Accordingly, rates of stone ballast/stone grits, inclusive of
transit fee at the prevailing rates, were approved for SoRs of Allahabad and
Pratapgarh Circles of the Department for the period up to November 2009. In
March 2010, the Government waived off levy of the transit fee on forest
produce.

Scrutiny of records of the office of the Chief Engineer (CE), Allahabad Zone,

Allahabad revealed (March 2011) that although the transit fee was waived off

in March 2010, the concomitant reduction on that account was not effected in

the SoRs of these two circles till March 2011. As a result, the estimates
sanctioned after March 2010 continued to include the element of transit fee.

Scrutiny of vouchers, relating to works sanctioned during 2010-11, revealed

that stone grit!” were used in various works in the Allahabad and Pratapgarh

circles and payments on that account were made inclusive of transit fee. This
had resulted in extra payment of = 1.89 crore to the contractors as detailed in

the (Appendix 3.5).

3.1.6 Undue aid to the contractors

Payment of secured advances, in violation of the contract conditions, led
to undue aid of € 94.17 crore to the contractors and undue benefit of
= 2.23 crore as interest.

In the light of judgment'* (July 2006) of Hon’ble High Court, Allahabad,
Lucknow Bench, the Government modified (January 2007) the existing tender
procedure to bring transparency in the tendering process and introduced Model
Bid Documents’? (MBDs) and directed the Engineer-in-Chief, Public Works
Department (PWD) to ensure its compliance scrupulously throughout the
State. The document provided, inter-alia that the contractor would be eligible
to receive mobilization and equipment advance against production of
unconditional Bank Guarantee of any Scheduled Bank and subject to
submission of invoices for purchase of machineries to be put into use on
works relating to the contracts.

Scrutiny of records (February and March 2011) of Executive Engineer (EE),
Construction Division-I and Provincial Division of PWD, Lucknow and
further information collected (May to July 2011) from 13 other divisions”
revealed that undue aid of हैं 94.17 crore was provided to contractors as
secured advance”! without any provision in the MBD. The secured advances
were recovered after the period ranging from one to 14 months. Further, the
State Government paid interest of = 2.23 crore on हैं 94.17 crore, calculated at
the average rate of 7.53 per cent for the year 2009-10, the rate at which the
State Government went in for commercial borrowings during 2008-09, 2009-
10 and 2010-11 (Appendix 3.6). The avoidable interest outgo was particularly
significant as 96 per cent of the State’s borrowing during 2009-10 were spent
on debt servicing of loans taken by it during the past years. Thus, the secured
advances facilitated the contractors to carry out the works awarded to them by
utilising the secured advances. This was irregular as the contractors were
liable to execute the works awarded from their own resources and payments
were to be made only after the execution of works to the entire satisfaction of
the concerned authorities.

3.2 Audit against propriety and cases of expenditure without adequate

justification

Authorisation of expenditure from public funds is to be guided by the
principles of propriety and efficiency of public expenditure. Authorities
empowered to incur expenditure are expected to enforce the same vigilance as
a person of ordinary prudence would exercise in respect of his own money and
should enforce financial order and strict economy at every step. Audit detected
instances of impropriety and extra expenditure.

3.2.1 Unfruitful expenditure

Laser Land Levelers (LLLs), purchased at a cost of हैं 2.49 crore,
without assessing their feasibility, were lying idle, rendering the
expenditure incurred thereon unfruitful. Besides, the objective of
ensuring appropriate and minimum use of water in irrigating the
agriculture fields was also not achieved.

Planning entails assessment of requirements after comprehensive and credible
surveys.

With a view to ensuring appropriate and minimum use of water in irrigating
the agriculture fields, the Government sanctioned (March 2009) ₹ 2.94 crore
for purchase of 99 Laser Land Levelers under Kushal Jal Prabandhan Yojna.
These levelers were to be used in tractors for leveling farmers’ agriculture
land on demand and on payment of hire charges of € 400 per hour. Scrutiny of
records of the Director, Agriculture, Lucknow in July 2009 and further
information collected from 17 Bhoomi Sanrkashan Adhikari”® (BSAs) and
nine District Agriculture Officers’’(DAOs) during April 2011 revealed that
the Director, Agriculture, Lucknow purchased (March 2009) 84 levelers for
use by the farmers in 70 districts and 15 for Government Agriculture Farms at
a total cost of = 2.49 crore”* without assessing their feasibility and supplied 84
LLLs (March 2009 to July 2009) to the BSAs of 70 districts. However, all the

In violation of the orders of the Supreme Court, Ghaziabad

of the Hon’ble Supreme Court to maintain the Status quo, GDA placed orders
for electrical items rendering the expenditure of = 15.92 crore on supplied

and agitation of farmers. It was further stated that a proposal to transfer these

3.2.6 Excess payment

Excess payment of हैं 3.48 crore was made to the contractor due to
preparation of estimate by allowing inadmissible Schedule of Rates.

Financial rules*’ provide that to facilitate the preparation of estimates, as also
to serve as a guide in deciding rates in connection with contract agreements, a
Schedule of Rate (SoR) for each kind of work commonly executed should be
maintained in the division and kept up-to-date. It should be prepared on the
basis of the rates prevailing in each locality and necessary analysis of the rates
for each description of work and for the varying conditions thereof should, so
far as may be practicable, be recorded. The rates entered in estimates should
generally agree with the SoRs but where, for any cause the latter are not
considered sufficient, the deviation should be explained in detail in the report
on the estimate.

Scrutiny of records (March 2011) of the Executive Engineer (EE), Irrigation
Construction Division (ICD), Mauranipur, Jhansi revealed that the
construction of spillway and earthen embankment was taken up under ‘Pahari
dam modernisation project’ at 75 metre downstream of existing dam situated
in Tehsil Mauranipur of Jhansi district. SoR of Jhansi Circle is applicable at
this site. An estimate of = 96.30 crore for the project was sanctioned (February
2009) by the Chief Engineer (Betwa Project), Jhansi and an agreement** was
executed (February 2009) by the Superintending Engineer, Irrigation
Construction Circle, Mahoba for the project.

Scrutiny further revealed that while preparing the estimate of the above work
the SoR applicable to Chitrakoot Dham revenue circle, Banda was taken into
account instead of the SoR applicable for Jhansi Circle, under which the site
of work was located. Total 59 items were sanctioned in the estimate, out of
which rates of 41 items were higher than the rates of Jhansi circle, which had
resulted an overestimation of work by € 4.16 crore.

Resultantly, an excess payment of हैं 3.48 crore on nine items under the
agreements was made to the contractor as of June 2011 (Appendix 3.9).

On being pointed out (March 2011) in audit, the EE replied that the work of
‘Pahari dam project’ was being executed on river 'Dhasan' which is situated on

© Paragraph 523 of FHB Vol. VI.
# Q1/SE/2008-09 at a cost of % 90.84 crore.

95


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
—_ 34 94 आह 4991 3३3७3), ४३333.30 43.

the borders of Jhansi and Banda Circles. In the estimate the rates for material
were taken from the SoR of Jhansi Circle but the rates for labour component
were taken from the SoR of Banda Circle as the labour was engaged from
Banda Circle. The reply was not acceptable as the rates included in the
estimate were approved for completed item of works and not for labour and
material separately. Further, the rates included in the estimate should be of
SoR of the concerned circle and not of the other circle.

Thus, an excess payment of हैं 3.48 crore was made to the contractor due to
adoption of higher SoR of other circle.

During discussion (November 2011), the Government admitted the facts and
assured to constitute an enquiry in the case.

3.2.7 Avoidable expenditure

Non-adherence to original alignment for the construction of Algin-
Charsari bund led to avoidable expenditure of % 8.98 crore.

The Government accorded (January 2005) administrative sanction for
construction of Algin-Charsari Bund at a cost of € 14.58 crore on the left bank
of river Ghagra to provide protection from flood to the people of Gonda,
Bahraich and Barabanki districts. The project was approved by Ganga Flood
Control Commission (GFCC), Patna (January 2005). The Chief Engineer
(CE), Sharda Sahayak approved the alignment (September 2005) of
Algin-Charsari Bund (km 0.000 to km 44.800) and sanctioned (December
2005) an estimate of = 11.36 crore for earth work.

Scrutiny of records (March 2011) of Executive Engineer (EE), Flood Works
Division, Gonda, revealed that the alignment of the Bund (km 0.000 to km
44.800) was changed” (April 2006) and the length was increased by 7.600 km
(km 0.000 to km 52.400) by CE without approval of GFCC*, Patna (August
2011). In the changed alignment, the bund at km 10 to km 20 was projected
towards the river as a result the bund became vulnerable and was damaged at
km 15.350 which scoured (August 2010) from km 14.945 to km 16.500 due to
floods. It was further noticed that the division incurred an expenditure of
र₹ 8.15 crore on flood protection works at km 10 to km 20 and started the
construction of a ring bund costing हैं 1.39 crore for protection from flood and
further damage to the bund. The work was left incomplete after incurring an
expenditure of हैं 83.11 lakh (March 2011). A committee constituted in
September 2010, headed by Engineer-in-chief (Planning and design) U.P.,

3.2.8 Idle investment on pumping machinery

Purchase of pumps, electric motors and accessories, not synchronised
with the completion of electric distribution system and construction of
sub-station rendered the investment of हैं 2.65 crore idle.

As per Financial Rules*' of the Government, purchase of store material far in
advance should be avoided. Further, in a project constituting electrical,
mechanical and civil works, it is required that the purchase of pumps, motors
and electrical appliances should be synchronised with the construction of sub-
station and its distribution system and civil works to ensure their
commissioning in a time bound manner.

The Government accorded (January 2008) administrative approval and
financial sanction for the Chaudhari Charan Singh Tanda Pump Canal
Renovation Project at a cost of = 9.76 crore to increase water discharge
capacity of Tanda Pump Nahar from 300 to 450 cusecs with the scheduled
date of completion by March 2010. The Technical Sanction for the revised
project for the mechanical and electrical works was accorded in September
2009 for = 8.62 crore. In the revised project, हैं 4.77 crore and % 3.85 crore
were sanctioned for electrical and mechanical works, respectively. Besides,
the civil works of % 5.03 crore was to be carried out by the Irrigation Division,
Tanda, Ambedkar Nagar by March 2010.

Scrutiny of records of the Executive Engineer, Lift Irrigation Division,
Faizabad (Division) and further information collected revealed (June 2010 and
April 2011) that a sum of % 3.41 crore was provided to the Executive
Engineer, Electrical Work's Division, Madhyanchal Vidyut Vitran Nigam
Limited, Faizabad against the revised estimate of = 2.77 crore during 2007-09
for the construction of 33 KV independent distribution system from Akbarpur
to Mahripur without assigning any specific time-schedule for completion of
work. The division procured six pumps/motors® and accessories between
February 2008 to March 2010 at a cost of = 2.65 crore without synchronising

3.2.9 Unfruitful expenditure due to non-completion/delayed completion
of jails

Failure of the department to release funds timely and finalise the scope of

buildings for jails in six districts, despite revisions of completion
schedules two times and cost escalation ranging between 23 and 145 per
cent defeating the prime objective of de-congesting the existing jails.

To de-congest the existing jails, the State Government (Government)
sanctioned (between March 2003 and March 2006) ₹ 68.50 crore for
construction of five jail buildings in five newly created districts under a
centrally sponsored scheme”. The cost, under the scheme, was to be shared in
the ratio of 75 and 25 by the Governments of India (Gol) and the State. In
addition to above, the construction of jail building in Maharajganj, sanctioned
in May 1998 for = 9.93 crore (€ 50 lakh was released with the sanction) by the
State Government, was also included under the scheme in November 2005 to
accommodate 3240 additional prisoners in six district jails. The Government
nominated Uttar Pradesh Rajkiya Nirman Nigam (UPRNN) as the executing
agency with completion schedules between March 2007 and November 2008
(Appendix3.10). The Director General, Jail Administration and Reforms
Services, Lucknow (DG) was to monitor the quality and completion of works.

defeated.

(March 2011) infructuous.

3.2.11 Excess payment on suspected consumption

Adoption of different norms for material in construction of same nature
of works resulted in excess payment of 1.63 crore.

Technical sanction®’ to an estimate means that the proposals are structurally
sound and the estimates are accurately calculated and based on adequate and
reliable data. Per cubic metre requirement of loose quantity of stone ballast of
the same quarry for the same type of work should always be same.

Scrutiny of records and information collected (October 2009 and July 2011
respectively) from the Executive Engineer, Construction Division-I, Unnao,
revealed that the Government sanctioned (June 2008) widening and
strengthening of two stretches of Sandila-Rasoolabad-Chakalvansi 1091 and
Safipur-Miyaganj 10907 in District Unnao. The Chief Engineer (CE), Central
Zone, Lucknow accorded (June 2008) technical sanctions to the estimates
taking the consumption norms of 1.29 cum loose quantity of stone ballast per
cum in Water Bound Macadam (WBM) works. The stone ballast was taken
from Kabrai quarry situated in Mahoba district.

The two contract bonds for Sandila-Rasoolabad-Chakalvansi road and one for
Safipur-Miyaganj road were executed (July 2008) with the same contractor
for WBM work, which were executed accordingly. However, in other two
roads‘, the CE accorded the technical sanctions by taking consumption norms
of 1.21 cum of loose quantity of stone ballast per cum in WBM work of the
same quarry. This resulted in excess consumption of 0.08 cum” loose
quantity of stone ballast for every cubic meter compacted quantity of WBM
with extra expenditure of = 1.63 crore (Appendix 3.11). Moreover, possibility

३.3 Failure of oversight/governance

The Government has an obligation to improve the quality of life of the people
for which it works towards fulfillment of certain goals in the area of health,
education, development and upgradation of infrastructure and public services
etc. However, Audit noticed instances where funds released by Government
for creating public assets for the benefit of the community remained
unutilised/ blocked and/or proved unfruitful/ unproductive due to
indecisiveness, lack of administrative oversight and concerted action at
various levels. A few cases have been discussed below.

3.3.1 Loss of % 79.93 crore due to non-realisation of the cost of land

Cost of land allotted for housing schemes was not realised even after
lapse of 11 to 19 years.

Budget Manual”’ provides that any substantial amount due for realisation
against another Government, local body or other outside party should not be
allowed to remain outstanding for a long time.

The Government decided (January 1987) to transfer land” to Central and
State Government Undertakings, Development Authorities, Housing and
Development Boards, Local Bodies, Harijan Evam Nirbal Warg Awas Nigam
and registered co-operative housing societies at two thirds of the circle rates.

Scrutiny of records (May 2008) of the Competent Authority, Urban Land
Ceiling, Kanpur and information collected (April 2011) revealed that Urban
Land Ceiling, Kanpur transferred 12.55 lakh square metre land” of village
Bara Sirohi, Kanpur to Kanpur Development Authority (KDA) for Jawahar
Puram Avas Yojna (First and Second Phase) for development and sale to
private parties. The amount of % 0.68 crore for the first phase was incorrectly
computed by Urban Land Ceiling, Kanpur at the rate of = one plus solacium
(i.e. = 11.99) per square metre, instead of prevailing circle rate. The total cost
of the land worked out to = 81.43 crore (First phase: हैं 26.55 crore and second
phase: % 54.88 crore) at the prevailing circle rates” fixed by District

Magistrate, Kanpur. The Government ordered (October 2002 and October

amount of ¢ 79.93 crore was yet to be realised as of October 2011.

Besides, KDA had also not shown the amount as liability in its balance sheet as
of March 2010. Thus, the Government failed to recover the balance amount from
KDA even after 11 to 19 years of handing over of land for phase I and II which
had resulted in non realisation of Government dues of 79.93 crore.

The Urban Land Ceiling, Kanpur stated (April 2011) that the matter had been
referred to the Government for realization of the balance amount. The
Government accepted (October 2011) the facts.

Thus, due to inaction on the part of the Government, ~ 79.93 crore remained
unrealized for 11 to 19 years.

3.3.3. Rupees 1.28 crore were spent for unintended purposes leading to
misappropriation

Employment Guarantee Scheme (MNREGS) led payment to
contractors of ~ 1.28 crore for unintended purposes.

Guidelines of Mahatma Gandhi National Rural Employment Guarantee
Scheme, September 2005, (MNREGS) stipulates that funds allocated under
the Scheme should not be used for purposes other than those stipulated under
any circumstances and engagement of contractors and machinery are not
allowed. The objective of the scheme is to enhance livelihood security in rural
areas by providing at least 100 days guaranteed Wage employment in a
financial year to every household whose adult members volunteer to do
unskilled manual work.

With a view to generate employment for 2,33,850 persondays Chief
Development Officer (CDO) Maharajganj sanctioned &% 2.72 crore
(March 2008) for increasing the capacity of 23 drains and हैं 55.40 lakh (April
2008) for restoring Wasulah Guide Bundh under the Scheme. The CDO
released 75 per cent of the sanctioned amount of & 2.46 crore*(March and
April 2008), for both the projects to generate 1,75,540 persondays
employment and the rest 25 per cent amount was to be released after
utilisation of first installment.“

Scrutiny of records (August 2010) of Executive Engineer (EE) Irrigation
Division-II Mahrajganj and subsequent information collected (May 2011)
revealed that out of = 2.46 crore received, only = 90.45 lakh was spent on the
proposed work under MNREGS which generated only 66,831 persondays of
employment and % 27.41 lakh was refunded (February 2009) to the CDO as
further work on this Project was not needed. Further scrutiny revealed that in

*S & 2.04 crore for restoration of drains and & 0.42 crore for Wasulah Guide Bundh.
“ The balance 25 per cent was not released.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

violation of the provisions of the Scheme, the balance amount of
% 1.28* crore, intended for generating employment of 1,08,709*° persondays
under the scheme, was paid to the contractors to clear the liabilities

agreements/work orders by the then EE. Thus, the funds received under
MNREGS were misappropriated and used for unintended purposes and

3.3.4 Misutilisation of Government revenues

Misutilisation of € 2.24 crore relating to trade tax, income tax and royalty
deducted from the contractors' bills for the clearance of liabilities.

The UP Budget Manual*’ and Financial rules** provide that an allotment
should be appropriated only for the objects for which it is sanctioned.
Diversion of funds allotted through Cash Credit Limit (CCL) without the
authorisation of competent authority is to be treated as financial irregularity
and dealt with accordingly. In order to ensure that the codal provisions are
strictly followed, the State Government imposed a further restriction (August
2004) that deductions made on account of trade tax, income tax and royalty
from the bills of the contractors of a particular work have to be remitted into
the treasury under proper head of account immediately against the allotment
of CCL for that work placed at the disposal of the Drawing and Disbursing
Officer (DDO). DDOs were also made responsible for excess payments on
account of levy of interest as well as penalty due to delay in remittance of the
income/trade tax recovered.

Scrutiny of the records of the Executive Engineer (EE), Flood Works

Division, Gonda revealed (March 2011) that CCL meant for remitting the

amount of deductions of % 2.24 crore®’ from the contractors' bills during

2009-10 on account of trade tax, income tax and royalty into the treasury was

misutilised for clearance of liabilities” created due to execution of works in

absence of budget allotment in previous years. EE incurred expenditure over

CCL of 2010-11 was remitted into treasury towards tax liabilities of 2009-10.

On being pointed out EE replied (March 2011) that the amount was utilised
for the payment of necessary works carried out during the flood. The reply
was in violation of the instant rule as the utilisation of amount deducted from
contractors bill on account of trade tax, income tax and royalty for payment
of liabilities was not only unauthorised but also created an additional liability
of 2.24 crore and paved the way for imposition of penalty”! of ₹ three crore
by Trade Tax Department.

Further, the Superintending Engineer, Investigation & Planning (Flood)
Circle, Lucknow rejected (November 2010) the demand of budget made by
the Chief Engineer (Saryu-I), Irrigation Department, Faizabad for the payment
of above remittances into treasury with the remarks that no budget could be
allotted for the purpose. As per provisions of UP Budget Manual, excess
expenditure should be regularised on the basis of the recommendation of the
Committee on Public Accounts, by presenting to the Legislative Assembly
demands for excess grants as required under Article 205 (1) (b) of the
constitution.

Thus, the CCL of रे 2.24 crore was misutilised for clearance of the liabilities
of previous years and an additional liability to that extent was created for
which no budgetary allotments had been made. Payment of € 20 lakh from the
CCL of 2010-11 towards tax liabilities of 2009-10 was also irregular.

The Government accepted (November 2011) the facts and stated that
departmental action was being initiated against the concerned EE and action
will be taken to deposit the amounts in respective heads of accounts at the
earliest.

3.3.5 Infructuous expenditure

Non-obtaining of No Objection Certificate from Bhabha Atomic
Research Centre resulted in non-commissioning of the cancer unit
valuing हैं 5.99 crore.

To check and treat the widespread cases of oral cancer, caused due to use of
tobacco, in Mainpuri and neighboring districts, the Chief Minister, Uttar

3.3.6 Infructuous expenditure

Infructuous expenditure of हैं 23.22 crore on civil works and loss of

To provide health services by super specialists to the people of Basti and
Poorvanchal, the Government of Uttar Pradesh sanctioned (March 1991) a

the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries (OPEC) as OPEC had to
share the expenditure”’ with the State Government. The original sanctioned
(March 1991) cost of % 21.68 crore, revised to = 45.95 crore in November

were awarded to Uttar Pradesh Rajkiya Nirman Nigam (UPRNN). As per the
bond executed with UPRNN by the Directorate of Medical and Health
Services, UP, Lucknow (Directorate), the civil works were to be completed
within 36 months of the commencement (September 1991) of the work with
damages”® for delay in completion”. Date of completion of civil work was
revised to March 2001 and again to March 2007. The first phase (100 bed
wards/hospital) and second phase (200 bed wards/hospital) were made
functional in 1997 and 1998 respectively. The posts of medical, para-medical
and other employees for the first two phases were sanctioned in October 1997
and March 1998 respectively and the proposal for the medical, para-medical
and other staff for the third phase (200 bed wards/hospital) was sent to the
Directorate in December 2003. Of the total sanctioned cost of = 52.04 crore,
% 46.95 crore were released up to March 2001 and the balance % 5.09 crore
was released in 2005-06. The State Government sanctioned (November 2006)

schedule of completion.

3.3.7 Wasteful expenditure

Commencement of work without acquiring the required land, rendered
an expenditure of € 23.18 crore wasteful.

Paragraph 378 of FHB Volume VI provides that no work should commence on
a land unless it has been handed over by a responsible Government officer. So
far as land earmarked for military purposes in the cantonment area is
concerned, the proposal for its occupation should be made to the cantonment
authorities in an appropriate manner for onward submission and approval of
the Government of India as envisaged in paragraph 374 ibid.

The Government approved (February 2009) the departmental proposal to
construct a six-lane ring road (5.200 km) from Gomti Barrage of Lohia Path to

On being pointed out in audit, the Department stated (May 2011) that
alignment of road was approved and work was commenced keeping in view
the general perception that land of the rivers and drains belonged to the State
Government. It was further stated that one end of the constructed road had
been connected to the existing main road and on the remaining portion old
road was available for transport.

The reply was not correct as financial rules do not permit commencement of
work on general perception and without having clear title and legal possession
of the land. Besides, ‘No Objection Certificate’ was also not obtained from
the Defence Authorities. Further, construction of road in a small segment did
not serve the purpose of diversion of traffic through a shorter route and
reduction of traffic congestion as both the ends are yet to be connected (March
2011). Thus, lack of proper planning in executing the work rendered an
expenditure of = 23.18 crore wasteful as of March 2011.

The Government accepted (November 2011) the facts and stated that efforts
were being made to take over the land from the Army.

3.4 Persistent and pervasive irregularities

An irregularity is considered persistent if it occurs year after year. It becomes
pervasive when it is prevailing in the entire system. Recurrence of
irregularities, despite being pointed out in earlier audits is not only indicative
of non-seriousness on the part of the executive but is also an indication of lack
of effective monitoring. This in turn encourages willful deviations from
observance of rules and regulations and results in weakening of the
administrative structure. The case reported in audit about persistent
irregularities has been discussed below.

3.4.1 Unauthorised and unfruitful expenditure

Execution of construction works of Rapti Link Channel (RLC) in
violation of the orders of the Government led to unauthorised and
unfruitful expenditure of € 2.21 crore.

Mention was made in paragraph 3.1.7.4 in the Report of the Comptroller and
Auditor General of India (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2008 regarding
irregular expenditure of ~ 93.84 crore incurred on Rapti System. The
objective of 21.400 km long Rapti Link Channel (RLC) was to feed Rapti
Main Canal from Saryu Main Canal under Saryu Canal Project of which 1.185
km! channel has been left to be constructed. Despite orders issued by the
Government in 1999 and 2002 for stopping the work of Rapti system, the
department incurred = 93.84 crore on Rapti System as of July 2008.

Scrutiny of records (June 2010 and May 2011) of Executive Engineer (EE),
Saryu Nahar Division-IV, Bahraich revealed that in violation of the orders of
the Government (February 2003) the Executive Engineer (EE) executed six
agreements (February 2009) for the construction of RLC from Km 2.880 to
3.990 in District Bahraich at a cost of € 2.09 crore. An expenditure of 2.21
crore was incurred on the said works from the funds allotted for Saryu Canal
Project (SCP) (Phase-I and II) between 2008 and 2011. Works of RLC was
neither approved by Expenditure Finance Committee (EFC) nor
administrative and financial sanctions for the work was accorded by the
Government. Thus, execution of works of RLC was not only in violation of
the Government order but also the expenditure on it was unfruitful as RLC
was still incomplete (November 2011).

Audit of financial transactions, test checked in one department of the
Government and their field functionaries showed instance of fraudulent
payments as discussed in the following paragraph:

3.5.1 Fraudulent drawal of pay and allowances

Failure to verify the genuineness of the transfer order by the Executive
Engineer, Construction Division, Bridges and Roads, Gorakhpur, as
ordered by the Engineer-in-Chief, Public Works Department, not only
facilitated the joining of two fake employees but also resulted in
fraudulent drawal of salaries of 3.73 lakh.

Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C), Public Works Department ordered (July 2004) all
the Zonal Chief Engineers to direct all the Executive Engineers (EEs) under
their jurisdiction to verify the transfer orders relating to Group D employees
(Peon, Beldar, Mate, Chawkidar, Work Agent, etc.) with the office of E-in-C
before allowing them to join on transfer basis. The E-in-C also ordered that
concerned EEs would be responsible if they allowed the fake employees to
join their offices without verification of the genuineness of transfer orders.

Scrutiny of records (June 2011) of the Executive Engineer, Construction
Division, Bridges and Roads, Gorakhpur revealed that on the basis of fake
transfer orders issued (May 2009 and November 2009) by the office of E-in-C
two fake employees!” reported to Construction Division, Bridges and Roads,
Gorakhpur for joining on the basis of last pay certificates (LPCs) issued by
Executive Engineer, Provincial Division, Unnao and Executive Engineer,
Temporary Division (PMGSY), Kannauj (September 2009 and January 2010).
The Executive Engineer, Construction Division, Bridges and Roads,
Gorakhpur violating the departmental orders, did not verify the transfer orders
and LPCs with the office of E-in-C and allowed two fake employees to join
his office on 25.09.2009 and 11.01.2010 respectively. After elapse of 20
months (September 2009 to April 2011) and 16 months (January 2010 to

*°5 Shri Satish Kumar Tiwari, Mate and Shri Anjani Kumar Singh, Mate

Chapter IV

Vv CHIEF CONTROLLING OFFICER BASED
AUDIT OF A GOVERNMENT DEPARTMENT

4.1 Integrated audit of Horticulture and Food Processing Department

Executive Summary

Horticulture and Food Processing Department is responsible for holistic

growth in productivity and quality of horticultural crops through latest
technologies and extension of food processing methods to generate
employment and enhance farm income.

In Chief Controlling Officer based audit of the Department, the period of
2006-11 was covered to examine the economy, efficiency and effectiveness of
significant activities keeping in view its mandate. The major audit findings
are as under:

e The Perspective Plan for the entire period (2006-11) was not prepared

and Annual Action Plans for 2006-11 were prepared without inputs from
district/field level agencies, resulting in fixing up of unrealistic targets.

e Mechanism for collecting reliable and realistic data for assessment of
horticultural area, production and productivity at District as well as at
State level was not developed by the Department.

e Against the Plan Budget provision (2006-11) of हैं 559.01 crore, the State
Government released only € 327.71 crore (59 per cent).

e Against the approved outlay (2006-11) of ₹ 669.83 crore, Gol released
only € 322.05 crore (48 per cent) and the Department could utilise only
53 to 87 per cent of the available funds. Failure of the Department to
utilise the original allotment resulted in non-release of approved outlay
in the subsequent years.

e An avoidable expenditure of हैं 1.17 crore was incurred (2008-11) on
purchase of Garlic seeds.

e An amount of & 43.60 crore, parked in PLA (2007-09), was booked as
final expenditure. In March 2009, ₹ 23.90 crore was withdrawn from
PLA and deposited into the revenue head.

e Irregular assistance of % 8.88 crore was provided to the beneficiaries for
vegetable seed production.

e An avoidable assistance of * 19.69 crore was provided to the
beneficiaries (2006-10) without ensuring survival of Medicinal, Spices
and Aromatic crops.

e Irregular expenditure of % 1.53 crore was incurred (2006-11) on printing
of publicity material.

e Idle expenditure of हैं 2.06 crore was incurred (2006-11) on incomplete
works at Government seed farms, nurseries and parks.

u7


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Uttar Pradesh (geographical area: 2.41 lakh Square km) is an agro-based State.
Its 70 per cent population is dependent on agriculture, of those, 90 per cent are
small and marginal farmers (majority belonging to Scheduled Caste). The
share of the State was 447.15 lakh MT (26 per cent) of the country’s total
horticulture production (2004-05) of 1698 lakh MT. Only 20 per cent of the
fruits and vegetables produced in the State are processed and a large part (40
per cent) finds its way to other State. Due to inadequate infrastructure for post
harvesting and processing, large quantity of horticultural produce is wasted,
which adversely affects the income level of the farmers as well as State’s
economy.

The vision of the Horticulture and Food Processing Department (Department)
is to increase the productivity and quality of horticultural crops through use of
latest technology and extension of food processing techniques (viz.
establishment of pack houses, ripening chambers, controlled atmosphere
storage, refer vans and mobile processing units) in order to restrict wastage of
horticultural produce by creating post harvesting infrastructure.

Horticulture development and food processing has been an ideal option for
diversifying agriculture and heralding a Golden Revolution. Such a revolution
will empower the people by generating employment opportunities, improving
economic conditions and providing nutritional security. The Department has
been selected for CCO! based audit to assess the impact of implementation of
various schemes and to ascertain the development of area under horticulture,
productivity issues and adequacy of post harvesting facilities.

Principal Secretary, Horticulture and Food Processing Department is
responsible for overall implementation and monitoring of various schemes and
programmes launched by the Government of India (Gol) and Government of
UP (GoUP). At the Department level, Director, Horticulture and Food
Processing heads, the Department and is assisted by an Additional Director
and four Joint Directors. At district level, District Horticulture Officer (DHO)/
Superintendent, Government Gardens assisted by Horticulture Inspectors are
responsible for implementation and monitoring of the schemes /programmes.

The objectives of CCO based audit were to ascertain whether:

e adequate institutional capacity to achieve the desired objectives was in
existence;

' Chief Controlling Officer

us


Chapter IV : Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government Department

e compliance with relevant rules, laws and regulations in discharge of the
mandated functions was ensured; and

e the schemes and programmes launched by the Gol and GoUP were
implemented and also whether the delivery of services was efficient and
effective.

Following were the sources of criteria adopted for the CCO based audit:

e Guidelines issued by Gol and the GoUP;
e Budget Manual and financial rules:

e Budget documents, perspective plan, annual action plans and performance
budget;
e Departmental policies/rules and regulations;

e Procedures prescribed for monitoring, evaluation and internal audit; and

e Reports on joint physical inspection and interviews with the beneficiaries.

Audit covered 32 units {14 units of category’-H (80 per cent), 14 units of
category-M (40 percent) and 4 units of category-—L (20 per cent)
(Appendix 4.1)} out of 73 units of the Department selected through Simple
Random Sampling without Replacement (SRSWOR) method. The units
selected for CCO based audit are denoted in the map.
