Exit Conference was held with the Joint Secretary, Horticulture and Food
Processing Department on 8 February 2012 during which the audit findings
and recommendations were discussed. The reply received from the
Government has been suitably incorporated at relevant places in the report.

Evaluation of the activities of an entity by conducting a performance audit is
possible when requisite information and records are furnished by the audited
entity to Audit. Despite the assurance given by the Secretary of the
Department during the entry conference held on 24 May 2011 that all
information and records, required by Audit, would be made available, the
records and replies to audit memoranda were not produced to the audit team.
A letter was addressed to the Director demi-officially (27 July 2011) regarding
non-production of records and the matter was taken up further by the Principal
Accountant General with Principal Secretary of the Department (7 September
and 12 October 2011). However, the information/ records related to schemes,
tender documents and post harvest activities and replies to 644 audit
memoranda were largely not furnished as of February 2012 (Appendix 4.2).

The non- production of records and non-furnishing of information to various
audit memoranda limited the scope of audit to that extent.

Audit Findings

Every organisation needs to have sound infrastructure, sufficient manpower
and funds to manage and achieve its mandate. This ensures soundness and
appropriateness of the internal systems and controls in its key areas of
activities and drives the organisation towards its objectives in an economical,
efficient and effective manner. Some of the areas, where the activity of the
Department was found weak, are discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:
4.1.8.1 Planning

A Perspective Plan (PP) and Annual Action Plan (AAP) is essential for
systematic implementation of any scheme. The schemes for improving
Horticulture and Food Processing require baseline survey for assessing the
status of horticultural production, its potential and demand, post-harvesting
facilities and processing centres before preparation of these plans.

120


Chapter IV : Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government Department

Scrutiny of the records at the Directorate and districts levels revealed that
neither baseline survey was conducted nor PP was prepared. AAPs prepared
and submitted by the districts were revised at the Directorate level on the basis
of data on area and productivity of horticultural produce reported by the Board
of Revenue, Agriculture Statistics and Crop Insurance Department.
Consequently, physical targets fixed for the implementation of the schemes at
district level were largely not achieved as discussed in subsequent paragraphs.

Scrutiny further revealed that the data of area and production of Mango,
Guava, Potato and Tomato made available for the period 2006-07 to 2009-10,
collected and reported by the test checked districts to the Directorate and data
entered in the records maintained by the Directorate had substantial
differences as detailed in Appendix 4.3.

Thus, the participation of the implementing agencies at the grass root level
in the planning process was largely absent. As a result, most of the
infrastructural projects were implemented in an unsystematic manner which
retarded the progress of the schemes as discussed in subsequent paragraphs.

While accepting the facts, the Government stated (February 2012) that
proposal for creation of database of horticultural area and production had been
approved by the Planning Department and reliable data would be available
after physical verification.

4.1.8.2 Human Resources Management

Adequate human resource is a key element required for preparation,
implementation and monitoring of schemes/programmes.

Scrutiny of records of Directorate and test checked districts revealed shortage
of key functionaries at each level as detailed in Table 1.

(May 2011) by DHO, Kaushambi that physical verification of plantation in
fruit garden was not done due to shortage of staff resultantly subsequent
installments of € 0.36 crore of financial assistance was not released by 0110.

The Government while accepting the facts stated (February 2012) that
necessary action for filling up the vacant posts was under process. The
reorganisation of the Department was also in progress and one official would
be available at each block.

Thus, shortage of manpower at various levels adversely affected the
achievements of the targets of the schemes and programmes as envisaged.

4.1.8.3 Infrastructure Development

In order to formulate overall development of the horticulture sector post-
harvest management, processing and marketing of horticultural produce, a
network of infrastructural facilities was to be created.

To meet the developmental requirement of horticulture sector, Gol launched
(2005-06) State Horticulture Mission, jointly funded by the State Government,
in 45 districts® of the State and in remaining 28 districts, the State Government
launched (2006-2007) Professional Horticultural Development of Intensified
Area on the pattern of SHM.

Audit findings on the implementation of the schemes are discussed in

4.1.8.4 Monitoring and Evaluation
Monitoring

In the planning documents of SHM, MI, RKVY and PHDIA schemes, it was
provisioned that a State Level Committee would monitor the schemes in the
State and at regional levels, Deputy Director (DD) would monitor the
implementation of these schemes and verify 20 per cent of scheme activities.
DHOs of the concerned districts were solely responsible for implementation of
the schemes and their verification.

Scrutiny of the records by audit (May-June 2011) and information made
available by the Directorate revealed that under SHM 15 meetings (October
2005 to January 2011) were held at the State level. In case of MI and RKVY
schemes, only three State level meetings were held during 2006-11. Further
scrutiny revealed that no mention was made in the minutes regarding
monitoring of the scheme activities by the DDs and DHOs vis-a-vis the
prescribed percentage.

123


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011
—_ 34 94 आह 4991 3३3७3), ४३333.30 43.

Evaluation of
SHM was not
done and even
corrective
measures
initiated by the
State Planning
Institute were
not adopted.

Scrutiny of the records (April - September 2011) of the 11 DDs and 24 DHOs
of the test checked districts revealed that monitoring reports of inspection
were neither prepared by DDs nor by DHOs. In reply to Audit query (April -
September 2011), the concerned DDs/DHOs stated that the monitoring of the
schemes was done but no monitoring/inspection note was prepared. In the
absence of any documentation, the existence and adherence to prescribed
monitoring mechanism was not verifiable.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that directions
had been issued to all the Divisional Deputy Directors and District Officers for
monitoring and verification as per norms.

Evaluation

Guidelines of SHM provided that evaluation of the scheme was to be
conducted at the end of the Tenth plan period (March 2007). The State Level
Executive Committee (SLEC) of SHM in its Fifth meeting (January 2007)
initiated the process of selection of an agency for evaluation of the scheme
activities. Till the Sixteenth meeting of SLEC (January 2011), selection of the
agency was not finalised. Thus, the impact of the scheme was not evaluated by
any independent agency and therefore, the advantages of mid course
correction, if required, were denied.

State Planning Institute (SPI) of GoUP conducted (2009-10) evaluation of
PHDIA scheme by selecting five (Aligarh, Bahraich, Bijnor, Fatehpur and
Kanpur Dehat) out of 26 districts. The evaluation report inter alia pointed out
that only the people with resources benefited; survival of plantation was low
due to inferior quality of planting materials, late/short supply of plants and
seeds, rejuvenation of fruit garden against the norms, beneficiaries were not
provided assistance as per norms etc. SPI also evaluated (2009-10) MI scheme
by selecting five districts (Allahabad, Kaushambi, Meerut, Raebareli and
Saharanpur) of the State. SPI in its report stated (January 2010) that the
progress of the scheme was slow as beneficiaries lacked technical knowhow. It
also stated that norms to benefit Scheduled Caste/Scheduled Tribe population
were not adopted and only 54 per cent of the system (Drip and Sprinkler) were
established of which only 67 per cent were functional.

SHM was not evaluated at all and performance of other small schemes, which
were evaluated, was inadequate and no corrective measures initiated to rectify
the same come to notice during the period under review, defeating the very
purpose of evaluation.

The Government accepted the facts and stated (February 2012) that a
committee headed by Class -I officer would be constituted in each district for
verification of inputs viz., seeds, plants, fertilizer and chemicals etc. It was
also informed to Audit that payment would be released only after verification.

4.1.8.5 Internal Control

Internal control is a process by which an organisation directs its activities to
achieve its objectives effectively. Scrutiny of the records at the Directorate
level revealed that the Internal Audit Wing of the Directorate was required to
conduct annual audit of the district units. During 2006-11, the wing planned
internal audit of 95 units every year but only 34 to 70 units were actually
covered. The percentage of unaudited units increased from 32 to 64 in 2006-
07 and 2010-11 respectively.

Statutory audit of the Department was being conducted by the Principal
Accountant General (Civil Audit) UP, Allahabad. As of January 2012, 245
paragraphs (Money value: ~ 97.30 crore) of 91 Audit Inspection Reports
(2007-12) were outstanding.

Due to failure to conduct internal audit as planned and to take remedial action
on the observations of statutory audit, large number of objections/ issues
remained outstanding which diluted the oversight mechanism.

The Government while accepting the facts stated (February 2012) that due to
shortage of staff the targets were not achieved. It was also stated that annual
targets of internal audit would be ensured and a schedule would be made for
clearance of outstanding audit paragraphs of internal and statutory audit at
Directorate level.

For sound financial administration and control, it is essential that expenditure
conforms to financial rules, regulations and is in consonance with orders
issued by the competent authority. This not only prevents irregularities,
misappropriation and frauds but also helps in maintaining good financial
discipline. Some of the audit findings regarding non-compliance with rules
and regulations are discussed in the following paragraphs:

4.1.9.1 Financial Management
Preparation of Budget and its utilisation

Budget Manual of GoUP stipulates that the Budget Estimates (BEs) are to
be consolidated by the Controlling Offices based on the proposals
received from the subordinate offices and should be as accurate as
possible. The Directorate did not produce any record/ information regarding
the basis for preparation of BEs for the period 2006-11.

The details of the budget estimates proposed by the Department during
2006-11, sanctions and releases by the Government and expenditure incurred
there against are given in Table 3.

Irregular purchases of Garlic Seeds under SHM

Paragraph 260 (Appendix 18) of Financial Handbook (FHB) Volume V (Part
J) stipulates that purchases of non-rate contract items should be made through
tendering process and from prominent and registered firms. Besides, detailed
specifications should also be mentioned in the tender document to get more
competitive and fair rates.

In this regard the SLEC under the chairmanship of Agriculture Production
Commissioner (APC) also decided (June 2008) to ensure quality and for
uniformity in rates. A committee headed by the Director was to be constituted
for entering into agreement of quantity and rates for the supply of seeds,
planting material and other inputs through tendering.

Under the SHM, the Department was required to purchase certified Garlic
seeds for the “Establishment of Spice Plants Garden”. But due to non-
adherence to the financial rules, the purchases were made from different firms
(Appendix-4.4) and an avoidable expenditure of = 1.17 crore was incurred as
discussed below:

06) Scrutiny of records of the test checked districts revealed that during 2008-
09, 14 DHOs purchased 1,73,918 Kgs of Garlic seeds from different agencies
and at different rates. DHO, Meerut purchased the seeds (Variety: G-282) at
the rate of % 50 per kg from NHRDF* whereas other 13 DHOs purchased the
seeds (G-282) from HP Agro, NAFED, UP Khadi Board, UP Seeds
Corporation Limited ete. at rates ranging from % 69.40 to = 125 per kg. This
led to extra expenditure of % 81.03 lakh as detailed in Appendix 4.4.

(ii) During 2009-10, the Department invited rates from prominent firms for
supply of Garlic seeds. NHRDF, Nasik, Maharashtra offered the rate of = 50
per kg for supply of the seeds (G-282). This offer was accepted in the eleventh
meeting (3 March 2009) of SLEC. But, the Directorate, directed 30 September
2009, concerned districts to make purchase of Garlic seeds (G-282) from

* National Horticultural Research and Developmental Foundation (NHRDF)

127


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

To avoid lapse of

crore was

NHRDF though the sowing period for Rabi season was October-November
2009.

In the meantime, the Department also enlisted (17 June and 1 July 2009) nine
other firms for purchase of Garlic seeds (G-282) without prescribing the rates.

Scrutiny of records of the test checked districts revealed that 19 DHOs
purchased 1,00,424 kg Garlic seeds (G-282) from different firms at different
rates ranging between रैं 64 to हैं 125 per kg and incurred an excess expenditure
of हैं 26.79 lakh (Appendix 4.4) which was avoidable, if seeds would have
been purchased from NHRDF at the rate of हैं 50 per kg.

Thus, nominating the firms for supply of seeds at higher rates without the
consent of the SLEC and delaying the communication of the names of the
firms ready to supply at lower rates to the districts ultimately burdened the
public exchequer.

Gii) During 2010-11, DHOs of 15 districts purchased 32,061 kg Garlic
seeds (G-282) at the rate of हैं 120 per kg from NHRDF and incurred an
expenditure of हैं 38.47 lakh, though Himachal Pradesh Agro Industries
Corporation Limited (HPAICL) had offered (July 2010) the rate of € 90 per kg
to the Director and all DHOs. Thus, ignoring the offer of HPAICL, excess
expenditure of हैं 9.60 lakh was incurred by the concerned DHOs
(Appendix 4.4) on purchase of Garlic seeds (G-282).

The Government accepted the facts and stated (February 2012) that in future
centralised purchases would be made.

Thus, non-adherence to financial rules and lackadaisical approach of the
Department led to avoidable expenditure of € 1.17 crore.

Funds parked in PLA refunded to the Government

Paragraph 162 of the FHB Volume V (Part I) stipulates that money should not
be drawn from the Government Treasury unless it is required for immediate
disbursement.

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that हैं. 43.60 crore
(Appendix 4.5) sanctioned by the Government during 2006-08 for the
implementation of various schemes was withdrawn from the Treasury and
parked in the PLAs’ of UP Bhoomi Sudhar Nigam Limited (UPBSNL),
Lucknow and UP Scheduled Caste Finance and Development Corporation
Limited, Lucknow. The Department deposited (March 2009) ₹ 23.90 crore
into its receipt head of account (Major Head-0401) thereby showing it as
inflated departmental receipts to that extent. Only the balance % 19.70 crore
was released to the districts for implementation of the schemes.

Non-preparation of Bank Reconciliation Statements

Bank Reconciliation is an essential internal control mechanism that highlights
and explains the difference between bank balance shown in the bank statement
and the corresponding amount shown in the cash book at a particular point of
time. The preparation of reconciliation statement is mandatory for each DHO
operating schemes through bank accounts.

Scrutiny of the records of the test checked DHOs revealed that during
2006-11, DHOs (19) did not prepare Bank Reconciliation Statement of SHM
bank account despite huge differences between the closing balances of Cash
Book and those shown in the bank statements as detailed in Appendix 4.7.

Moreover, in offices of DHOs mentioned in Table 5, the cash books
(in 3 districts: main cash book and in 3 districts: Scheme specific cash book)
were not maintained.

The Department was implementing mainly 13 schemes (2006-11). Of these six
functional schemes, selected on the basis of expenditure, were examined and
results thereof are discussed in the following paragraphs:

4.1.10.1 State Horticulture Mission (SHM)

Gol launched (June 2005) National Horticulture Mission (NHM), a Centrally
Sponsored Scheme, for holistic development of horticulture by ensuring
horizontal and vertical linkages with active participation of all the
stakeholders. Gol revised (2007-08) the funding pattern (Gol: 85 and State:
15 per cent) and envisaged to increase the horticulture growth rate of the State
from 6.57 to 10.87 per cent under the Eleventh Five Year Plan (2007-12)
covering 45 districts and an additional area of 15.75 lakh hectare
(Appendix 4.10).

Objectives of the scheme

The main objective of the SHM was to provide holistic growth of the
horticulture sector through an area based regionally differentiated strategies

"Allahabad, Azamgath, Bahraich, Basti, Bijnor, Bulandsahr, Fatehpur, Ghazipur, Gorakhpur, Jaunpur, Jhansi, Kanshi

Ram Nagar, Kaushambi, Kushinagar, Lakhimpur Kheri, Lucknow, Mahoba, Mathura, Meerut, Mirzapur,
Moradabad, Muzaffarnagar, Raebareli, Saharanpur, Sant Kabir Nagar, Siddharth Nagar, Sonbhadra, Sultanpur and
Varanasi.

Vegetable Seed Production

Scheme of Vegetable Seed Production envisaged giving financial assistance to
farmers for producing seedlings for germination in such a manner that early
and late crop could be possible thereby avoiding glut and scarcity of produce.
The assistance was based on quantum of seedlings produced per hectare in a
given region (210 quintal/hectare (ha) in Eastern districts and 230 quintal in
Western districts). In case of pea seeds, prescribed quantity was 12 quintal/ha
for all the districts of the State. The assistance was to be provided after
assuring production of seeds as per norms and their certification (tagging) by
UP Seeds Certification Institute.

Scrutiny of records of the test checked districts revealed that the financial
assistance of & 8.88 crore (Potato: हैं 1.79 crore and Peas: ~ 7.09 crore) was
provided (20 districts'') to the beneficiaries during 2006-11 without assessing
the prescribed quantum of seed produced.

Thus, due to non-adoption of SHM norms, the fulfillment of intended
objectives of seed production was not ensured.

Establishment of New Gardens

The SHM envisaged bringing new areas under perennial fruit crops (Mango,
Guava, Litchi, Aonla, Bael, and Citrus), flowers (cut flowers, bulbous flowers
and loose flowers) spices (Chilly, Garlic, and Turmeric) and aromatic crops
(Patchouli, Geranium, Rosemary, and Damask Rose). It envisaged providing
assistance for cultivation spread over a period of three years in the ratio of
50:20:30, subject to survival rate of 75 per cent in the second year and
90 per cent in the third year of the new gardens.

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that the Department
released (2006-10) entire assistance of हैं 41.80 crore to the beneficiaries
of 39,308 ha in the first year itself against admissible amount of € 22.11 crore
(₹ 5,625 x 39,308) as per NHM guidelines.

This resulted in irregular release of assistance of € 19.69 crore (Medicinal:
& 0.83, Spices: हैं 15.17 and Aromatic: € 3.69 crore) for 39,308 ha of land
during 2006-10 without ensuring the survival of the plantation in the
subsequent years.

The Government accepted the facts and stated (February 2012) that future
assistance would be provided only after ensuring the survival of the plantation.

The above reply is indicative of the absence of monitoring, internal controls
and fulfilment of the objectives of the Mission.

Non- adoption of norms of financial assistance

The quantum of assistance was fixed (2005-06) as हैं 22,500 per ha for
perennial fruits (maximum 75 per cent) and हैं 11, 250 per ha for spices and
aromatic plants. The financial assistance of cultivation was normally for one
crop and was to be spread over a period of three years in the ratio of 50:20:30,
subject to survival rate of 75 per cent in second year and 90 per cent in the
third year. Gol revised (April 2010) the rates’? of financial assistance in the
ratio of 60:20:20

Post Harvest Management

Post harvest management was an important component of the SHM. Under the
programme processing and marketing of horticultural produce, creation of a
network of infrastructural facilities for storage, packaging, grading,
transportation, curing and ripening was essential for increasing the
marketability of the horticulture produce.

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that during 2006-11, the
Department made provision for 20 components in post harvest activities.
Further scrutiny revealed that in 13 components, achievement was less than
10 per cent.

Scrutiny of records of the test checked districts revealed that during 2006-11,
though provision was made and funds were available but achievement was
only between zero to 31 per cent (Appendix-4.14), This indicated that
allocation in the AAP was made without assessing the field level requirement
resulting in lack of support mechanism to add value to the farmers’ produce.

In reply, concerned DHOs stated (April — September 2011) that due to
non-sanctioning of the project by the Directorate and lack of interest shown by
the beneficiaries, achievement was lacking.

The reply is not acceptable as the post harvest activity was an important
component of the scheme to reduce the pilferages of the farmers produce and
proper publicity for generating awareness of the activities should have been
made. Further, timely approval of the projects should also have been ensured
by the Department.

4.1.10.2 | Rashtriya Krishi Vikas Yojna (RKVY)

With a view to achieve four per cent annual growth in horticulture and
agriculture sector during the eleventh plan period, Gol launched (May 2007)
RKVY, a centrally sponsored scheme (100 per cent), to enhance
agricultural/horticultural production by providing qualitative seeds and
planting material, agricultural equipment, assistance to State Seed Farms and
organising study tours for the farmers.

136


Chapter IV : Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government Department

Objectives of the Scheme

The main objectives of the scheme were to increase the availability of
improved seeds and elite planting material, expansion of area under high value
vegetables, improving horticultural technology, establishing convergence and.
synergy among multiple programmes, enhancing and strengthening the
technical knowledge of the farmers and to create opportunities for
employment for skilled and unskilled youth.

Financial Management

Agriculture Department was the nodal agency for RKVY and was also
responsible for preparing State Agriculture Plans (SAP), which inter alia
included horticulture development. The utilisation certificates and quarterly
physical & financial progress reports were to be furnished to the Department
of Agriculture and Cooperation, Government of India.

Allocation and expenditure

The scheme was divided into two streams. In Stream -I, 75 per cent of the
total allocation under the RKVY was earmarked for activities relating to
enhancement of horticultural production and under Stream - II, remaining
25 per cent allocation was to be utilised for strengthening of the existing State
sector schemes and filling the resource gaps. The funds were provided by the
Agriculture Department for implementation of the schemes as mentioned in
Table-7.

4.1.10.3 Micro-Irrigation (MI)

Gol launched (January 2006) Micro-Irrigation (MI) project, for harnessing
maximum benefits from available water resources and to enhance crop
productivity without affecting soil health.

Objectives of MI

The main objectives of MI were to increase the productivity of crops and
farmers’ income through improved technologies and to enhance the water use
efficiency.

Funding pattern

The expenditure on subsidy assistance was to be shared among Gol and State
Government in the ratio of 80:20. Funds to the tune of 50 per cent were to be
released directly to the State/Department on the basis of AAP. The remaining
amount was to be released subsequently on the basis of progress report and the
utilisation certificates submitted by the State/Department.

Financial Management

During 2005-06 to 2010-11 the Gol and State Government released
र₹ 22.04 crore and & 8.03 crore respectively for the scheme. According to
norms of funding, the State Government should have released % 5.51 crore but
released % 2.52 crore in excess of its share. The Department utilised
र₹ 17.07 crore as Gol share leaving a balance of % 4.97 crore in the bank
accounts of Directorate and DHOs. In case of State Government share, the
Department utilised only हैं 4.40 crore and remaining हैं 3.63 crore was
surrendered during 2006-11.

Thus, the Department blocked Gol funds and surrendered its own fund thereby
showing skewed financial management.

During the Exit Conference, the Government accepted the fact
(February 2012).

Drip and Sprinkler Irrigation

Drip irrigation technology involves irrigating plants at root zone through
emitters fitted on a network of pipes (mains, sub-mains and laterals). Water
requirement, age of plant, plant to plant spacing, soil type, water quality and
availability decides the choice of emitting system.

Sprinkler system is suitable for irrigating crops where the plant density is very
high, like cereals, pulses, seed spices and other field crops. In sprinkler
system, water is discharged under pressure in the air through a set of nozzles

140


Chapter IV : Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government Department

attached to a network of High Density Polythene Pipes (HDPE), simulating
rainfall.

4.1.10.4 National Mission on Medicinal Plants

Gol launched (March 2009) National Mission on Medicinal Plants (NMMP) a
Centrally sponsored scheme (100 per cent) during Eleventh Plan for
promotion of the medicinal plants in compact areas of the State to promote
cultivation of species critical to AYUSH systems of medicines in 41 districts
(Appendix-4.17) of the State. The major components of the NMMP were
establishment of nursery, capacity building of the farmer, post-harvest
management and creation of marketing infrastructure.

A State Level Steering Committee under the chairmanship of the APC of the
State was responsible for implementation of the scheme activities.

Objectives of NUMP

The main objectives of the scheme were to support cultivation of medicinal
plants; promote standardisation and quality assurance by adopting good
agricultural and collection practices; support setting up of processing
zones/clusters through convergence of cultivation, warehousing, value
addition and marketing; development of infrastructure for implementation and
certification mechanism for ensuring quality standards.

Financial Management

Gol guidelines stipulated that release of Gol was to be made only after
submission of utilisation of releases of previous years.

Infrastructure, हैं. 14.84 crore (2009-10: हैं 2.72 crore and 2010-11:
& 12.12 crore) was provisioned.

Scrutiny of the records of the Directorate revealed that against the targets for
2009-10 there was no achievement inspite of the availability of funds
(® 4.01 crore). The Gol released no funds for 2010-11 due to non-utilisation of
previous year’s funds.

For cultivation of Satavar, seed (4-5 years crop) was provided to the
beneficiary farmers by DHO, Bahraich (2008-09) and Superintendent Garden,
Jhansi (2009-10). During physical inspection (August/September 2011) it was
noticed that due to non-creation of marketing infrastructure for Satavar the

**CEMAP, Lucknow; CSA University of Agriculture, Kanpur, Deemed University, Allahabad and Deemed
University, Agra.

Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

beneficiary farmers were deprived of the competitive rate for their produce.
Thus, the impact of non-development of marketing infrastructure resulted into
non-harvesting of the crop by the beneficiaries.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that the scheme
would be implemented in only those districts where marketing infrastructure
was available.

4.1.10.5 Food Processing Industrial Development Training (FPIDT)

Large quantity of horticultural produce perishes in the State due to inadequate
processing facilities. To ensure the availability of fruits and vegetables
throughout the year and also ensuring that farmers get appropriate rates, the
horticultural produce was to be processed using scientific methods and for this
adequate training need to be provided to the Departmental officers and staff.

The State Government established (1992-93) one Government Food
Processing Industrial Institute at Lucknow, 10 Government Food Scientific
Training Centres and 101 Government Community Food Processing and
Training Centres in the State for imparting training to the farmers,
unemployed youths and women.

During 2006-11, = 4.54 crore was released for ‘Food Processing, Industrial
Development Training’ and an expenditure of रं 4.50 crore was incurred.

Organising training programmes

With a view to create awareness and provide training to rural farmers,
unemployed youth and women, 101 Government Community Food Processing
Training Institutes were initially set up in the State. Targets and achievements of
the training programmes in the Centres during 2006-11 is given in Table 10.

ee

budget allocation and expenditure remained unchanged; and

e During 2006-10, targets given for processing demonstration (fruits and
vegetables) decreased gradually and no targets were fixed for the year
2010-11.

Thus, due to decreasing trend in training, demonstration related to food
processing and preservation, the desired objectives were not achieved by the
Department whereas horticultural activities were on an increase through
various schemes.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that due to
policy decision of keeping one centre at each district headquarter, the number
of training institutes were reduced. However, no document was produced to
Audit in support of this contention.

4.1.10.6 Professional Horticultural Development of Intensified Area
(PHDIA)

To accelerate the horticultural development in 28 districts of the State, which
were not covered under SHM, the State Government launched (2006-07) the
scheme of “Professional Horticultural Development of Intensified Area” on
the pattern of SHIM. During 2010-11, the Directorate did not prepare any
AAP, nor any target was fixed for programme implementation and thereafter
(March 2010) the scheme was discontinued. Out of 28 districts covered under
the scheme, audit scrutiny was conducted in six”! sampled districts.

Financial Management

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that during 2006-07 to
2009-10, the State Government made budget provision of % 171.19 crore
against which ~ 163.12 crore was released. The Department incurred an
expenditure of % 152.38 crore and the balance amount of & 10.74 crore was
surrendered to the Government.

Despite closure The scheme was discontinued in March 2010. However scrutiny of records of
of the scheme the test checked districts revealed that in Lakhimpur दिला हैं 16.94 lakh was

till July 2011, despite closure of scheme in March 2010.

In the Exit Conference, the Government accepted (February 2012) the facts.

(September 2011) revealed that during 2007-10, the financial assistance of

Thus, the proper norms were not followed in the identification of the
beneficiary farmer which resulted in non-achievement of the objective.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that an inquiry
would be conducted in the matter and results intimated to Audit.

Establishment of new Gardens

One of the major objectives of the scheme was to expand areas under
cultivation through establishment of new gardens to enhance production and
productivity of horticulture crops. The programme ‘Establishment of New
Gardens’ envisaged assistance for bringing new areas under perennial fruit
crops.

The quantum of assistance was fixed as 75 per cent of the cost for perennial
fruits (maximum हैं 22, 500 per ha). The assistance of cultivation was normally
for one crop and was to be spread over a period of three years.

Scrutiny of the records of the test checked districts revealed (Appendix-4.18)
that, financial assistance of € 2.10 crore in the plantation year (2007-10) was
provided to the beneficiary farmers of six test checked districts. All the
concerned DHOs stated that due to non-release of funds and closure of scheme
from 2010-11, second and third instalments of assistance were not released.
DHO, Lakhimpur Kheri stated that second and third year maintenance
assistance was not provided because of low survival of plants.

Thus, due to lack of effective follow up action after providing assistance to the
beneficiaries and coordination between district units and the Directorate in
planning and monitoring of the scheme, the objective could not be fulfilled

thereby resulting in unfruitful expenditure of 2.10 crore.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that the residual
works would be completed by preparing projects under RKVY.

Strengthening of infrastructure

For providing basic amenities to the trainee farmers, the State Government
sanctioned (February 2010) ₹ 43.88 lakh for renovation of hostel rooms of
farmers, link road, kitchen-cum dining hall and library cum-training building
and the construction of boundary wall at Horticultural Research and Training
Centre (HRTC), Basti. The work of construction was awarded to
UP Samaj Kalyan Nirman Nigam, Lucknow (Nigam) and a Memorandum of
Understanding (MoU) was signed (March 2010) between Nigam and the Joint
Director, HRTC. An amount of % 37.77 lakh was provided (May-December
2010) to Nigam.

148


Chapter IV : Chief Controlling Officer based Audit of a Government Department
ee

Scrutiny of the records of HRTC revealed that only after partial completion,
the renovation and construction work was stopped (August 2010) and the
balance work had not re-commenced (January 2012).

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that incomplete
work of Samaj Kalyan Department would be reviewed in the meeting of APC.

4.1.10.7 Non-functioning of Post Harvest Training Dissemination Centres

With a view to enhance and strengthen the technology base and post harvest
techniques, post harvest training dissemination centres (Three Horticulture
Technology Dissemination Centres, Eight Horticulture Post Harvest
Technology Centres and Eight Horticulture Post Harvest Technology Sub
Centres) were established (Year: 2003) at a cost of € 6.96 crore in 18 districts
with the assistance of World Bank through Uttar Pradesh Diversified
Agriculture Support Project (UPDASP). In the centres, all the beneficiaries
were to be provided training in processing and preservation of the horticultural
produce to reduce wastage. Further, during 2007-11, the Department incurred
an expenditure of 0.35 crore for operationalising the centres.

Scrutiny of the records of the Directorate revealed that the centres were not
made functional due to non-operation of purchased machinery and equipment
(Cost: हैं 3.45 crore). An enquiry, conducted (June 2009) by the Directorate,
revealed that payment was released to the supplier firm of machinery and
equipment before its operationalisation and imparting of training to the
operators.

Scrutiny of the records and physical verification of eight” out of 18 test
checked districts revealed that the centres were non- functional and machinery
and equipment were lying idle which was also evident from the photographs.

Dissemination centre in Basti (Dated: 20.7.11)

» Basti, Gorakhpur, Jaunpur, Jhansi, Meerut, Muzaffarnagar, Saharanpur and Varanasi.

149


Audit Report (Civil) for the year ended 31 March 2011

Wasteful
expenditure of
%7.31 crore on
Post-harvest
training
dissemination
centres.

In reply, the concerned DHOs stated that due to non-deployment of operators
and non-availability of raw materials, the machinery and equipment were
lying idle.

Thus, the post-harvest training dissemination centres were not made
operational even after a lapse of eight years of their establishment and
incurring an expenditure of हैं 7.31 crore (Machinery and equipment:
र₹ 3.45 crore, Establishing of Centres: हैं 3.51 crore and Operationalisation:
% 0.35 crore), rendering the entire expenditure wasteful.

The Government accepted the fact and stated (February 2012) that due to
non-availability of running cost, the centres were non- functional. It was
further informed that recurring budget would be demanded for making the
machines operational.

The reply was not acceptable as the machinery and equipment were lying idle
for more than eight years and the Department made no effort to provide
budget to meet the running cost of the centres and deployment of operators for
operating the system resulting in wasteful expenditure of % 7.31 crore.

Horticulture and Food Processing, an important component of the State’s
economy was not given due importance by the State Government. Annual
Action Plans were prepared without basic data and proper evaluation. The
targets given to district units were not based on the priorities, potential and
climatic conditions of the districts. Consequently, the targets were not
achieved and unviable” schemes were included in successive plans.

The short release of funds against the approved planned budget provision and
non-utilisation of even available funds adversely affected implementation of
programmes/schemes. The shortage of manpower at various levels resulted in
non-monitoring of the scheme activities affecting the quality achievements of
the schemes and programmes.

Non-adherence to financial rules and lackadaisical approach of the
Department led to avoidable expenditure on purchase of Garlic seeds and Low
tunnel poly house. Effective steps to establish nurseries for producing
qualitative planting materials and seeds were not taken. Model/small nurseries
were sanctioned without proper examination or evaluation of projects.

Post-harvest management, which was essential for improving marketability of
horticultural produce, was not given due attention despite adequate provision
and availability of funds. The existing monitoring and evaluation system was
inadequate, resulting in poor execution of projects.

e Perspective Plan should be prepared. Annual Action Plans should be
prepared after due assessment of viable horticultural crops in the State
and after ascertaining field level requirements;

¢ = Proposed schemes/programmes should be based on actual requirements
and the capacity to execute them;

e  SHM should take proactive action to ensure timely utilisation of funds
released to implementing agencies;

Rural marketing facilities should be established and extended to all the
Blocks/Panchayats to enthuse farmers; and

Implementation of the Programme/schemes needs to be closely monitored
and evaluated.
